The Different Shades of Infinite Session Types Thanks: Supported by EPSRC EP/T014628/1 “Session Types for Reliable Distributed Systems”, by FCT PTDC/CCI-CIF/6453/2020 “Safe Concurrent Programming with Session Types” and by the LASIGE Research Unit UIDB/00408/2020 and UIDP/00408/2020.
Simon J. Gay, Diogo Poças, Vasco T. Vasconcelos

Abstract
Many type systems include infinite types. In session type systems, which are the focus of this paper, infinite types are important because they allow the specification of communication protocols that are unbounded in time. Usually infinite session types are introduced as simple finite-state expressions 𝗋𝖾𝖼 ​ X . T {\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{rec}}}\,{X}.{T}}} or by non-parametric equational definitions X ≐ T {{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}} . Alternatively, some systems of label- or value-dependent session types go beyond simple recursive types. However, leaving dependent types aside, there is a much richer world of infinite session types, ranging through various forms of parametric equational definitions, all the way to arbitrary infinite types in a coinductively defined space. We study infinite session types across a spectrum of shades of grey on the way to the bright light of general infinite types. We identify four points on the spectrum, characterised by different styles of equational definitions, and show that they form a strict hierarchy by establishing bidirectional correspondences with classes of automata: finite-state, 1-counter, pushdown and 2-counter. This allows us to establish decidability and undecidability results for the problems of type formation, type equivalence and duality in each class of types. We also consider previous work on context-free session types (and extend it to higher-order) and nested session types, and locate them on our spectrum of infinite types.



1
Introduction

Session types

[
20
,
21
,
24
,
41
]

are an established approach to specifying communication protocols, so that
protocol implementations can be verified by static typechecking or dynamic
monitoring. The simplest protocols are finite: for example,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{int}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{bool}}}}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}}}}
describes a protocol in which an integer is
received, then a boolean is sent, and that’s all. Most systems of session types,
however, include equi-recursive types for greater expressivity. A type that endlessly
repeats the simple send-receive protocol is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{int}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{bool}}}}}}.{X}}}}}}}}}
, which can also be specified by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{rec}}}\,{X}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{int}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{bool}}}}}}.{X}}}}}}}}}
. More realistic examples usually combine
recursion and choice, as in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}
such that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}\{\mathrm{go}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{int}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{bool}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}}}}}},\mathrm{quit}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}\}}}}}}
which offers a choice between
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{go}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{quit}}}
operations, each with its own protocol. A natural observation is
that session types look like finite-state automata, but some systems from the
literature go beyond the finite-state format: for example, context-free
session types
[
42
]
and nested session types

[
10
,
11
]
, as well as label-dependent session types

[
43
]
and value-dependent session types

[
44
]
.

Even without introducing dependent types, a range of definitional formats can be
considered for session types, presumably with varying degrees of expressivity,
but they have never been systematically studied. That is the aim of the present
paper. We consider various forms of parameterised equational definitions,
illustrated by six running examples. Because our formal system only has one base
type, the terminated channel type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
, the running examples simply use
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}

(or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
for context-free session types) as a representative basic message
type that could otherwise be
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{bool}}}
or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{int}}}
.

Our study of classes of infinite types should be generally applicable; we make
it concrete by concentrating on session types where (potential) infinite types
occur naturally.
For the sake of uniformity, all our non-finite session types are introduced by
equations, rather than, say,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{rec}}}
-types. Equations may be further
parameterized, thus accounting for types that go beyond recursive types. The
examples below illustrate the different kinds of parameterized equations we use.

Example 1

(No parameters)
.

Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{loop}}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
with equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{X}}}}}}
.
Intuitively
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{loop}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}\dots}}}
continuously outputs values of type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
.

Example 2

(One natural number parameter)
.

Assuming
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}
and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}
as the natural number constructors and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}

as a variable over natural numbers, type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
with
equations

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{inc}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{dump}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{inc}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{dump}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}

A sequence of
n

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{inc}}}
operations followed by a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{dump}}}

triggers a reply of
n

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
output messages.
1
1

1

The final
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
at

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
closes the channel and does not count as a message.

Example 3

(Context-free types)
.

With type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
used either to finish a session or to move to the next
operation, type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{tree}}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
with equation

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{leaf}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}},\mathrm{node}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X};{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}};{X}}}}}}\}}}}

The
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{leaf}}}
choice terminates the reception of a binary tree of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}

values and the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{node}}}
choice triggers the reception of a (left) tree,
followed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}
(root), followed by a (right) tree. Even though
the development in the rest of the paper considers higher-order types (where
messages may convey arbitrary types rather than
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
alone), for simplicity
our example is first-order.

Example 4

(One list parameter)
.

Assuming
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau}}
as symbols and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}
as a
variable over sequences of symbols (with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}
the empty sequence), type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
with equations

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{pop}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma\tau S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau\tau S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{pop}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\}}}

Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}}
records simple protocols composed of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}

messages. Symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}
in a parameter to a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}

denotes an output message and symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau}}
an input message. The
protocol behaves as a stack with two distinct push operations (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{addOut}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{addIn}}}
). The symbol (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau}}
) at top of
the stack determines whether a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathrm{pop}}}
operation triggers

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
,
respectively.

Example 5

(Nested types)
.

Taking

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\mathrm{in}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\mathrm{out}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\mathrm{in}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\mathrm{in}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\mathrm{in}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{pop}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{\alpha}}}\}}}

Example 6

(Two natural number parameters)
.

Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{N^{\prime}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{N^{\prime}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N^{\prime}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}

Informally, writing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}^{n}}}}
for a sequence of
n
output
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}

messages, these definitions give

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}}={}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}^{1}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}^{2}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}^{3}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\dots}}

It is intuitively clear that
Examples

2
,
5
,
4
and
6

cannot be expressed without parameters. It is perhaps less clear that each
definitional style in
Examples

1
,
2
,
4
and
6
is strictly more expressive than the
previous one. This is the main result of the paper. We establish a hierarchy
from
finite session types all the way up to non-computable types that have no representation at all. The latter certainly exist, because for every infinite binary expansion of a real number between zero and one there is a session type derived by mapping 0 to send and 1 to receive — and we know for cardinality reasons that almost all of these types are non-computable.

Our methodology is to develop the connection between session types and automata,
in particular between progressively more expressive definitional styles and
progressively more powerful classes of automata. We also consider the formal
language class corresponding to each class of automata, and the decidability of
important properties such as contractiveness, type formation, type equivalence and type duality. Our
results are summarised in the table below, establishing a hierarchy of session
types in parallel to the Chomsky hierarchy of languages, where by a 1-counter
language, we mean a language accepted by a (deterministic) 1-counter automaton
and where DCFL abbreviates deterministic context-free languages.
In the final row of the table we make it clear that it is impossible to give an
explicit example of a non-computable type or to even state the decision
problems.

Context-free and 1-counter types are incomparable. Essentially, both models lie between levels 2 and 3 of the Chomsky hierarchy and correspond to different restrictions of deterministic pushdown automata. Context-free types correspond to constraining automata with a single state, whereas 1-counter types correspond to constraining the stack to have a single symbol.

General languages

Our main contributions can be summarized as follows.

•

We propose three novel formal systems for representing session types (1-counter, pushdown, 2-counter), show that they are strictly more expressive than recursive session types, and that each system is strictly more expressive than the previous one (
Theorem

2
).

•

We show that nested session types
[
10
]
are
equivalent to pushdown session types (
Theorem

2
).

•

We introduce higher-order context-free session types and show that they
stand between recursive and pushdown types, strictly (
Theorem

2
).

•

We characterize each of the novel session types in our paper by a
corresponding class in the Chomsky hierarchy of languages. Notably, we show
that each model captures precisely the power of the corresponding class of
automata (
Theorem

5
).
This is in contrast with the results of Das et al.
[
10
]
, who only show (in one direction) that nested session types can be simulated by deterministic pushdown automata.

•

We prove that the problems of type formation, type equivalence and type
duality are decidable up to pushdown session types (
Theorems

10
,
11
and
12
), but undecidable
for 2-counter session types (
Theorem

13
). This implies, in
particular, that equivalence for higher-order context-free session types is
decidable. The decidability results are not entirely unexpected, given that
type equivalence for nested session types was recently shown to be decidable

[
10
]
, and that these are equivalent to pushdown
types. However,
our proofs
are independent of Das et al.
[
10
]
.

•

Finally, we show a technical result in formal language theory of independent interest: every (deterministic 1-counter, deterministic pushdown, deterministic 2-counter) automaton that accepts a prefix-closed language can be converted into an automaton with a single non-accepting state, which acts as a sink (
Theorem

4
).

Organization of the paper

In
Section

2
we introduce the various classes of session types. In

Section

3
we explain how to associate to each given type a
labelled infinite tree, as well as a set which we call the language of traces of
that type. We also state our main results on the strict hierarchy of types and
on how previously studied classes of types fit into this hierarchy
(
Theorem

2
). In
Section

4
we describe
how to convert a type into an automaton
accepting its traces. In
Section

5
we travel in the converse
direction, i.e., from an automata into the corresponding type, and present a
characterisation theorem of the different types in our hierarchy
(
Theorem

5
).
In
Sections

6
and
7
we provide the details in the proof of
Theorem

2
;
Section

6
proves the main hierarchy and
Section

7
proves the results for context-free and nested session types.
In
Section

8
we present
our main algorithmic results: type formation, type equivalence
and type duality are all decidable up to pushdown types (
Theorems

10
,
11
and
12
),
and undecidable for 2-counter types (
Theorem

13
).
In
Section

9
we give an overview of related work and
Section

10
concludes the paper.

2
Shades of types

This section introduces the various session types in a uniform framework.

Figure 1:
Finite and infinite types.

The finite world

Finite types are in
Fig.

1
. The syntax of types is
introduced via formation rules, paving the way for infinite types. Session types
comprise the terminated type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
, the input type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{T}}}.{U}}}
(input a value of
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and continue as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
), the output type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{T}}}.{U}}}
(output a value of
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and continue as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
), external choice
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
(receive a label

k\in L
and continue as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{k}}}
) and internal choice
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
(select a
label
k\in L
and continue as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{k}}}
). To avoid repeating similar rules,
we use the symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp}}
to denote either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}}
, and
the symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}
to denote either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}
.
At this point type equivalence is essentially syntactic equality, but the rule
format allows for seamless extensions to infinite settings.
Types, type equivalence and duality are all
standard
[
16
,
21
,
47
]
.
Note that rule
D-Msg
defines duality with respect to type equivalence:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{T}}}.{V}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{U}}}.{W}}}
are dual types iff the type being exchanged is the same (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
) and the continuations are dual (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\bot}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
).

For finite types all judgements in
Fig.

1
are
interpreted
inductively
.
For example, we can show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}})}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}}}}
is a type by
exhibiting a finite derivation ending with this judgement.

Recursive types. Extends

The recursive world

Recursive types suggest the first glimpse of infinity. The details are in

Fig.

2
. Recursion is given via equations, rather than

\mu
-types for example, for easier extension. Towards this end, we introduce
type constructors
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and equations of the form
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
. The
set of type constructors is finite. We further assume at most one
equation for each type, so that there are finitely many
type equations.
Every valid type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is required to be contractive, that is

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
. Contractiveness ensures that types reveal a type constructor after finitely many unfolds, and excludes undesirable cycles that don’t describe any behaviour, e.g. cycles of the form

\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\}
. Contractiveness is inductive: we look for
finite derivations for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
judgements.
A coinductive interpretation of the rules would allow to conclude
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\>\mathrm{contr}

given an equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}
.
In contrast, type formation, type equivalence and duality are now
interpreted
coinductively
.

For example, no finite derivation would allow showing that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{loop}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}
.
Instead we proceed by showing that set
\{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{X}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}\}
is

backward closed

[
37
]
for the rules for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{type}
in

Fig.

2
, given that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{X}}}
, the right-hand side of the equation for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
, is contractive.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}\;::=\;\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}\;\mid\;\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}

E-zL
)

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}

E-sL

;

The 1-counter world

The next step takes us to equations parameterised on natural numbers. The
details are in
Fig.

3
. Natural numbers are built from the
nullary constructor
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}
and the unary constructor
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}
. We discuss the
changes from the recursive world in
Fig.

2
.
Given a variable

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}
on natural numbers, to each type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
we associate
at most two equations,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. The rules for recursive types
are naturally adapted to 1-counter types.
Here again,
type formation requires a suitable notion of contractiveness to
exclude cycles of equations that never reach a type constructor, e.g. cycles of
the form

\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\}
. Notice that the right-hand-side of an equation

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
is not necessarily a type for it may contain natural
number variables (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}
in particular).
However, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}
is a natural number,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}
(that is,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
with occurrences of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}

replaced by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}
) should be a type (cf. rule

T-s
). Again, to prove that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}
, we show that the set
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{inc}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{dump}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}\}\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n}}}\>\mathrm{nat}\}
is
backward closed.

Higher-order context-free types. Extends

A little detour takes us to context-free session types, proposed by Thiemann and
Vasconcelos
[
42
]
(see also Almeida et
al.
[
1
]
). Here we follow the distilled
presentation of Almeida et al.
[
2
]
, extending to
the higher-order setting (that is, allowing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{T}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{T}}}
for an
arbitrary type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
instead of just basic type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
).
The syntax for context-free session types (presented in
Fig.

4
) slightly departs from the main classes analysed in this paper; the
distinguishing aspects are as follows.

•

There is a new type constructor for sequential composition of session
types: the sequential composition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{U}}}
.

•

Type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
is replaced by a new type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
with a distinct behaviour.
Intuitively,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
is used to finish a session type, while
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
merely
moves to the next operation.

•

The constructors for sending and receiving are now simply
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{T}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{T}}}
, rather than
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{T}}}.{U}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{T}}}.{U}}}
.
3
3

3

Traditional
(first-order) context-free session types restrict messages to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}
, with
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
representing a basic type.

In order to align the presentation with the other classes of types, we use
equations rather than
\mu
-types as in the original work.

We discuss the main differences with respect to recursive types (
Fig.

2
).
For
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
to be a type under equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
, the right-hand side
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
must
be contractive, meaning that successive unfoldings either reach
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
or one
of the type constructs
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}
after finitely
many steps. This excludes non-types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
such as those defined under equations

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}};{X}}}}}}
, or
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}}}}
.
Contractiveness for sequential composition makes use of a new ‘is terminated’
predicate. Judgement
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\checkmark}
denotes a type that exibits no behaviour.
Terminated types are composed solely of constructors
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and
sequential composition.

On what concerns type equivalence, the first three rules (
T-Skip
to
T-Semi
) are
the congruence rules for the new type constructors. The last six rules
constitute the novelty of context-free types.
Sequential composition provides an associative monoidal structure, with
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}

acting as the identity (rules
E-NeutL
and
E-AssocL
). Rule

E-DistL
introduces distributivity of choice over sequencing.
The definition is again coinductive:
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
looks at the top constructors
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
. If either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
are sequential compositions, then
one of the six left or six right (not shown) rules apply.

A formulation of type equivalence that explicitly incorporates the rules of an
equivalence relation would allow reducing the number of rules while simplifying
the remaining ones. For example the four
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
-is-neutral rules would be reduced to
two axioms:
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}};{T}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
.
Unfortunately, scaling this approach to the coinductive setting would make every
element related to every
other
[
9
,
15
]

[
35
, Section 21.4]
.

Strings

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}\;::=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma s}}

New type contractive rules (
ind.
)
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}}

(
C-z
)

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma s}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}}

(
C-s
)

New type formation rules (
coind.
)
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{type}

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{type}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}}

(
T-z
)

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma s}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}}

(
T-s
)

New type equivalence rules (
coind.
)
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\>\mathrm{contr}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}

(
E-zL
)

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma s}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}

(
E-sL
)

Figure 5:
Pushdown types. Extends
Fig.

2
; removes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
; adds

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. Right versions of rules
E-zL
and
E-sL
omitted.

The pushdown world

The next extension replaces natural numbers by finite sequences
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}
of
symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}
taken from a given stack alphabet. The details are in

Fig.

5
. We use
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}
to denote the empty sequence. The extension from 1-counter is straightforward.
Parameters to type
constructors are now
sequences of symbols, rather than natural numbers; all the rest remains the
same.
Once again, to show that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}
, we proceed coinductively.

Nested session types

New type contractiveness rules (
ind.
)
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}}

(
C-s
)

New type formation rules (
coind.
)
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{type}

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}}

(
T-s
)

New type equivalence rules (
coind.
)
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}

\displaystyle\frac{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X\langle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}\quad\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{[}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{/}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{]}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}}

(
E-ConsL
)

Figure 6:
Nested types. Extends
Fig.

2
;
removes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
; adds
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. Right
version of rule
E-ConsL
omitted.

A class of types that turns out to be equivalent to pushdown types was recently
proposed by Das et al.
[
10
]
.
The main idea is to have type constructors that are applied not to natural numbers or to sequences of symbols
but to types themselves; and to let type constructors take a variable (but fixed) number of parameters. The syntax rules for nested session types is given in
Fig.

6
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
denotes a variable on types, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\alpha}}}
a possibly empty sequence of variables (once again,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}
denotes the empty sequence).
There are two differences with respect to 1-counter and pushdown types
(
Figs.

3
and
5
): on the one hand type
constructors are now applied not to natural numbers or to sequences of symbols
but to types themselves; on the other hand, type constructors take a variable
(but fixed) number of parameters, so that each type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
has an associated arity
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Type constructors are unfolded according to an equational definition of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{n}}}
are distinct type variables that
parameterise the type definition.

The 2-counter world

2-counter types extend the 1-counter types by introducing equations
parameterised on two natural numbers, rather than one. The new rules are a
straightforward adaptation of those in
Fig.

3
for 1-counter
types and are thus omitted.
To show that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{type}
, we proceed coinductively.

The infinite world

The final destination takes us to arbitrary, coinductive, infinite types. The details are in

Fig.

1
, except that all judgements not explicitly marked are
taken coinductively.
No equations (of any sort) are needed, just plain
infinite types.
We also allow choices with an
infinite number of branches.

Infinite types arise by interpreting the syntax rules coinductively, which gives rise to potentially infinite chains of interactions. The structure of these arbitrary, coinductively defined, infinite types does not need to follow any pattern (e.g. it does not need to repeat itself), and arguably, the best way to think about these objects are as labelled infinite trees (
Section

3
). Such objects do not have in general a finite representation (or finite encoding), which can be shown by a simple cardinality argument (
Lemma

5
). Hence the need for finding suitable subclasses of infinite types that can be represented and can be used in practice.

We can think of a type in two possible ways: as (one of) its representation(s), which is great for practical purposes as we can reason about types by reasoning about their representations; or as the underlying, possibly infinite, coinductive object which is being represented, which is suitable for developing a theory of types, in particular for comparing different models with one another.

Embedding context-free types into infinite types

In order to compare context-free session types with the other classes in our
hierarchy, we must convert context-free types into infinite types. We do this by
defining an embedding
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
(
Fig.

4
), where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a context-free session type
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is a corresponding infinite session type. The rules for the embedding
essentially unfold equational definitions, sequential composition, and
non-terminal occurrences of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
, until a lone
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
or one of the type
constructs
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}
is found. This takes
finitely many steps due to contractiveness. Type
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
appears either from a
lone
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
, or from a message
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T}}}
without a continuation.

Theorem 1

(Embedding)
.

1.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a context-free type and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is an infinite type.

2.

For every context-free type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
.

3.

Suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. Then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
.

Proof.

Sketched in
Appendix

A
.
∎

To be absolutely precise, we could explicitly define an embedding from each of the shades of types into the class of infinite (coinductive) types, in order to compare them with each other. However, for most cases this embedding is obvious and follows from the type formation rules. Only for context-free types, whose syntax is significantly different, did we feel the need to provide the rules for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
.

3
Types, trees and traces

It should be clear that the constructions defined in
Section

2
form
some sort of type hierarchy; this section studies the hierarchy. In any case,
every type lives in the largest universe; that of arbitrary, coinductively
defined, infinite types.

To each type one can associate a labelled infinite
tree
[
15
,
35
]
. This tree
can in turn be expressed by the language of words encoding its paths.
Let
\mathbb{L}
be the set of labels used in choice types.
Following Pierce
[
35
, Definition 21.2.1]
, a tree is
a partial function

t\in(\{\mathsf{d},\mathsf{c}\}\cup\mathbb{L})^{*}\rightarrow\{\mathsf{end},?,!,\&_{L},\oplus_{L}\mid L\subseteq\mathbb{L}\}
subject to the following constraints (below,
\pi
ranges over strings of symbols whereas
\sigma
ranges over symbols):

•

t(\varepsilon)
is defined;

•

if
t(\pi\sigma)
is defined, then
t(\pi)
is defined;

•

if
t(\pi)={}?
or
t(\pi)={}!
, then
t(\pi\sigma)
is defined for
\sigma\in\{\mathsf{d},\mathsf{c}\}
and undefined for all other
\sigma
;

•

if
t(\pi)=\&_{L}
or
t(\pi)=\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt_{L}
, then
t(\pi\sigma)
is defined for
\sigma\in L
and undefined for all other
\sigma
;

•

if
t(\pi)=\mathsf{end}
, then
t(\pi\sigma)
is undefined for all
\sigma
.

The labels
\mathsf{d}
and
\mathsf{c}
are abbreviations for
data
and
continuation
, corresponding to the components of a session type.

If all sets
L
in a tree are finite, the tree is finitely branching.
The tree generated by a (finite or infinite) type is coinductively defined as follows.

\displaystyle\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T_{\mathsf{d}}}}}.{T_{\mathsf{c}}}}})(\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle\ \sharp

\displaystyle\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}})(\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle\ \star_{L}

\displaystyle\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T_{\mathsf{d}}}}}.{T_{\mathsf{c}}}}})(\mathsf{d}\pi)=

\displaystyle\ \operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathsf{d}}}})(\pi)

\displaystyle\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}})(\ell\pi)=

\displaystyle\ \operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\ell}}})(\pi)

\displaystyle\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T_{\mathsf{d}}}}}.{T_{\mathsf{c}}}}})(\mathsf{c}\pi)=

\displaystyle\ \operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathsf{c}}}})(\pi)

\displaystyle\operatorname{treeof}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}})(\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{end}

A
path
in a tree
t
is a word obtained by combining the symbols in the
domain and the range of
t
. Given a symbol
\sigma\in\{?,!,\&_{L},\oplus_{L}\mid L\subseteq\mathbb{L}\}
in the codomain of
T
(but different from
\mathsf{end}
), and a symbol
\tau\in\{\mathsf{d},\mathsf{c}\}\cup\mathbb{L}
, let
\langle{\sigma},{\tau}\rangle
denote the combination of both symbols, viewed as a letter over the alphabet
\{?,!,\&_{L},\oplus_{L}\mid L\subseteq\mathbb{L}\}\times(\{\mathsf{d},\mathsf{c}\}\cup\mathbb{L})
. For simplicity in exposition, we often drop the angular brackets and the subscript
L
on the label set, and write, for example,
?\mathsf{c}
instead of
\langle{?},{\mathsf{c}}\rangle
,
\oplus l
instead of
\langle{\oplus_{L}},{l}\rangle
, etc.

Given a string
\pi
in the domain of a tree
t
, we can define the word

\operatorname{path}_{t}(\pi)
recursively as
\operatorname{path}_{t}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
and

\operatorname{path}_{t}(\pi\tau)=\operatorname{path}_{t}(\pi)\cdot\langle{t(\pi)},{\tau}\rangle
. We say that a string

\pi
is terminal wrt to
t
if
t(\pi)=\mathsf{end}
. For terminal strings, we
can further define
\overline{\operatorname{path}}_{t}(\pi)=\operatorname{path}_{t}(\pi)\cdot\mathsf{end}
.

Finally, we can define the language of (the paths in) a tree
t
as the set

\{\operatorname{path}_{t}(\pi)\mid\pi\in\operatorname{dom}(t)\}\cup\{\overline{\operatorname{path}}_{t}(\pi)\mid\pi\in\operatorname{dom}(t),\pi\text{ is terminal wrt }t\}
.
The language of (the traces of) a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, denoted by
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
, is the
language of
\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
.
Note that the traces of types are defined over the following alphabet.

\Sigma=\{?,!,\&_{L},\oplus_{L}\mid L\subseteq\mathbb{L}\}\times(\{\mathsf{d},\mathsf{c}\}\cup\mathbb{L})\cup\{\mathsf{end}\}

(1)

!
\mathsf{end}

\mathsf{end}
!
\mathsf{end}
\vdots
\mathsf{d}
\mathsf{c}

\mathsf{c}

\mathsf{c}

depicts (a finite fragment of) the tree corresponding to

Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}}
(

2
) describes an interaction that keeps
track of a counter.
Finite fragments of the corresponding tree and language are depicted in

.
Type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{tree}}}}
(

3
) describes the reception of a binary tree
of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
values.
Finite fragments of the corresponding tree and language are
depicted in
Fig.

.

In the above examples, the language
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is closed under prefixes. This holds for a general type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, since elements of
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
correspond to paths in
\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
.

Proposition 1
.

\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is prefix closed, that is, if
w\in\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
and
u
is a
prefix of
w
, then
u\in\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
.

Another immediate observation is that
\operatorname{treeof}
(resp.
\mathcal{L}
) is an embedding from the class of all types to the class of all trees (resp. all languages).

Proposition 2
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
be two types. The following are equivalent:

1.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
;

2.

\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})=\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
;

3.

\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})=\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
.

Proposition

2
tells us that two types are equivalent iff they have the same traces. Note that, in general, trace equivalence is a notion weaker than bisimulation
[
37
]
. However, both notions coincide for deterministic transition systems. The syntax of (infinite) session types is in fact deterministic (e.g. given a label
\ell
for a choice, there can only be one type that continues from
\&\ell
), which explains our result.

Section

2
introduces eight classes of types. We now distinguish them
by means of subscripts:
finite types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{f}}}}
,
Fig.

1
),
recursive types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{r}}}}
,
Fig.

2
),
1-counter types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{1}}}}
,
Fig.

3
),
context-free types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
,
Fig.

4
),
pushdown types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{p}}}}
,
Fig.

5
),
nested types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{n}}}}
,
Fig.

6
),
2-counter types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{2}}}}
) and
coinductive, infinite types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{\infty}}}}
,
Fig.

1
with rules interpreted
coinductively).
To each class of types we introduce the corresponding class of languages. For example,

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}
is the set
\{\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})\mid{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{r}}}}\}
. The strict hierarchy
result is as follows:

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{f}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{1}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{2}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{\infty}}

(2)

We remark that the last step in the chain of strict inclusions is obtained by a cardinality argument, since the set
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{\infty}}
is uncountable. This shows an even stronger statement:
for any finite representation system (including the systems
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{f}}
to

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{2}}
, as well as
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}
), there is an
infinite, uncountable
set of types that cannot be represented by that system.

We now turn our attention to nested types (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{n}}}}
) which turn out to be
equivalent to pushdown types, and further establish
equivalent sub-hierarchies inside both classes, parameterised by the
‘complexity’ of the corresponding representations. For pushdown session types, a
natural measure of complexity is the number of type constructors required to represent a given type. This number can be arbitrarily large, but
always finite. For a given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{n}
denote the subset corresponding to those types that can be represented with at most
n
type constructors. When
n=0
, there are no constructors, and we can only represent finite types. As
n
increases, so does the expressivity of our constructions, and we have the infinite chain of inclusions

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{f}}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{0}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}.

Similarly, for nested session types we can define a hierarchy by looking at the arities of the type constructors used. For a given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{n}
denote the subset corresponding to the nested session types whose type constructors have arity at most
n
. When
n=0
all type constructors are constant, and we recover the class of recursive types. As
n
increases, so does the expressivity, and we also have an infinite chain of inclusions

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{0}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}.

It turns out that these hierarchies are one and the same (with the exception of
the bottom level), so that we have (
Appendix

C
)

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{f}}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{0}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{0}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}.

(3)

Higher-order context-free types
(denoted by
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}
)
lie between levels
0
and
1
in the sub-hierarchies above, i.e., they can represent recursive types, and can be
represented by pushdown session types using at most one type constructor, or
equivalently, by nested session types with either constant or unary type
constructors, so that we have (
Appendix

C
)

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{1}.

(4)

Regarding the inclusion
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}
, we actually have a stronger observation. Context-free session types are included in pushdown session types
which have only one type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
, and where
the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
accounts for the only occurrence of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
. The latter means that the type ends
iff the state
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is reached, that is, iff the stack is
empty. Thus, we can intuitively think of context-free session types as
pushdown types with a single constructor and an empty stack
acceptance criterion.
This observation points to the fact that the qualifier ‘context-free’ in the so
called context-free session types is a misnomer, a remark that is not unheard of

[
10
]
.

The hierarchy that puts in context all the classes of types studied in this
paper is summarized in the result below.

Theorem 2

(Inclusions)
.

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{f}}

=

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{0}

\subsetneq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}

=

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{0}

\subsetneq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{1}}

\subsetneq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}

=

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}

\subsetneq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{2}}

\subsetneq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{\infty}}

\subsetneq

\subseteq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}

\subsetneq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}

\ =

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{1}

\subseteq

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{2}

\ =

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{2}

\subseteq

\cdots

4
From types to automata

This section describes procedures to convert types in different levels of the
hierarchy (recursive systems, 1-counter, pushdown and 2-counter) into automata
at the same level. All constructions follow the same guiding principles, so we
focus on the bottom level of the hierarchy (recursive systems) and then
highlight the main differences as we advance in the hierarchy.

All automata that we consider in this paper are
deterministic
and

total
, i.e., the transition functions are such that any input word has a
well-defined, unique computation path. We use the alphabet
\Sigma
defined
in (
1
). As standard references in automata theory we
mention the book by Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
]
and Valiant’s PhD thesis
[
45
]
.

Recursive types and finite-state automata

Following the usual notation, a (deterministic)
finite-state automaton
is
given by a set
Q
of states, with a specified initial state
q_{0}\in Q
, a
transition function
\delta:Q\times\Sigma\rightarrow Q
, and a set
A\subseteq Q
of accepting states. Given a finite word
a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}
, its execution by the automaton yields the sequence of states
s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
where
s_{0}=q_{0}
and
s_{i+1}=\delta(s_{i},a_{i+1})
.
We say that a word is accepted by the automaton if its execution ends in an accepting state.

Suppose we are given a system of recursive equations
\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{i}}}}\}_{i\in I}
over a
variable set
\mathcal{X}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}},\ldots\}
. Our first step is to
convert this system into a normal form in which every right-hand side is either a
variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
, or a single application of one of the type constructors, i.e., one
of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{X}}}.{Y}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{X}}}.{Y}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{X_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{X_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
. We can do this by introducing fresh, intermediate
variables as needed. Essentially, whenever we have an equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{T_{1}}}}.{T_{2}}}}}}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}
are not variables, we add two new variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime\prime}}}
, replace the above equation by
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{X^{\prime}}}}.{X^{\prime\prime}}}}}}}
, and add two new equations
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime\prime}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}}
. The process is the same for the other type constructors. By doing this repeatedly, we “break down” a long equation into many small equations. The number of new variables is linear in the encoding size of the original representation.

Given such a system, we construct a finite-state automaton (over the alphabet
\Sigma
) as follows. The
automaton has a state
q_{X}
for every type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
, and two additional
states: an ‘end’ state
q_{\mathsf{end}}
and an ‘error’ state
q_{\mathsf{error}}
. The
transitions from
q_{\mathsf{error}}
are described by

q_{\mathsf{error}}\overset{a}{\rightarrow}q_{\mathsf{error}}
for every symbol
a
.
Similarly, the transitions at
q_{\mathsf{end}}
are described by

q_{\mathsf{end}}\overset{a}{\rightarrow}q_{\mathsf{error}}
for every symbol
a
. The
transitions at state
q_{X}
are given by the corresponding equation for variable

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
, in the obvious way. Some examples:

•

Suppose our system contains the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}
. Then we have an

\varepsilon
-transition given by
q_{X}\overset{\varepsilon}{\rightarrow}q_{Y}
.

•

Suppose our system contains the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{Y}}}.{Z}}}}}}
. Then we
have the reading transitions
q_{X}\overset{!\mathsf{d}}{\rightarrow}q_{Y}
,

q_{X}\overset{!\mathsf{c}}{\rightarrow}q_{Z}
, and

q_{X}\overset{a}{\rightarrow}q_{\mathsf{error}}
for any
a\neq\ !\mathsf{d},!\mathsf{c}
.

•

Suppose our system contains the equation

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\{l\colon X,m\colon Y\}}}}
. Then we have the reading
transitions
q_{X}\overset{\hskip-0.3014pt\oplus\hskip-0.3014ptl}{\rightarrow}q_{X}
,

q_{X}\overset{\hskip-0.3014pt\oplus\hskip-0.3014ptm}{\rightarrow}q_{Y}
and

q_{X}\overset{a}{\rightarrow}q_{\mathsf{error}}
for any

a\neq\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057ptl,\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057ptm
.

•

Suppose our system contains the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
. Then we have the reading moves
q_{X}\overset{\mathsf{end}}{\rightarrow}q_{\mathsf{end}}
and
q_{X}\overset{a}{\rightarrow}q_{\mathsf{error}}
for any
a\neq\mathsf{end}
.

We define all states other than
q_{\mathsf{error}}
to be accepting states.
4
4

4

We need all states to be accepting, since we might need to look at finite traces to distinguish between two types. For example,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{a}\colon X\}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{b}\colon Y\}}}}
define non-equivalent types that have no finite terminating paths.
Notice that
the finite-state automaton described above is an automaton with possible

\varepsilon
-moves. Although, by definition, deterministic finite-state automata do not permit
\varepsilon
-moves, in our case paths of
\varepsilon
-moves are uniquely determined and either reach a state without outgoing
\varepsilon
-transitions, or become stuck in a loop
5
5

5

In this case, the system of equations is not contractive and does not define a type.
. We can convert the given automaton into an equivalent automaton without
\varepsilon
-moves by ‘shortcutting’ such moves. Formally, suppose a state
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
has an outgoing
\varepsilon
-transition to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}
; by construction, it is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
’s only outgoing transition. Assuming
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}
are different states, we can change every transition entering
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and make it enter
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}
instead; finally, we can remove state
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
(hence removing the
\varepsilon
-transition from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
). If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
’s outgoing
\varepsilon
-transition loops to itself, we can just remove this transition and treat
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
as a state from which no transitions are possible.

q_{X}
q_{Y}
q_{\mathsf{end}}
!\mathsf{c}
!\mathsf{d}
\mathsf{end}
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{X}}}}}}

Figure 10:
An automaton that defines
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{loop}}}}
with initial state
q_{X}
. All depicted states are accepting.

We show in
Fig.

10
the automaton that corresponds to type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{loop}}}}
(
Example

1
). Here we adopt the convention that every missing transition points to
q_{\mathsf{error}}
which is not shown. In our examples, all depicted states are accepting, so we omit the usual double circle notation.

1-counter types and automata

We augment the definition of finite-state automata into the definition of
1-counter automata as follows. We now have a partially defined transition
function
\delta:Q\times\{\mathsf{z},\mathsf{s}\}\times(\{\varepsilon\}\cup\Sigma)\rightarrow\{=,+,-\}\times Q
. The first argument of
\delta
corresponds to the current
machine state. The second argument of
\delta
indicates whether the counter
currently has value zero (
\mathsf{z}
) or some positive number (
\mathsf{s}
). Note
that we cannot directly read the counter value, only whether it is non-zero. The
third argument can be either a symbol in
\Sigma
(which is used for reading
moves), or
\varepsilon
(which is used for
\varepsilon
-moves). The output of

\delta
is given by a new machine state, and additionally, a counter operation,
which can be either
=
(no change),
+
(increment by one) or
-
(decrement by
one).
6
6

6

Of course, one has to be careful with the operation of
decrementing when the counter value is zero. One can exclude such
possibilities at the syntactic level, which is the case if automata are built
from well formed 1-counter types.
We are solely interested in
deterministic, total
transition functions, meaning that for each combination
(q,t)\in Q\times\{\mathsf{z},\mathsf{s}\}
, either

•

\delta(q,t,\varepsilon)
is undefined, and
\delta(q,t,a)
is defined for all
a\in\Sigma
(so-called reading mode) or

•

\delta(q,t,\varepsilon)
is defined, and
\delta(q,t,a)
is undefined for all
a\in\Sigma
(so-called
\varepsilon
-mode).

Intuitively, at a reading mode we
must read the next input symbol, whereas at an
\varepsilon
-mode we cannot read the next input symbol (but we can change the value of the counter and the current state). A configuration is given by a pair
(q,n)\in Q\times\mathbb{N}
, where
q
denotes the current state and
n
the current
value of the counter. Given
w
in
\Sigma^{*}
, a derivation
(q,n)\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
is a sequence of moves specified by the transition rules, that leads from
(q,n)
to
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
, and, in the process, reads the word
w
. Note that, for the same word
w
, there might be several configurations
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
for which
(q,n)\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
; all these lie in a unique path of
\varepsilon
-moves.

Similarly to finite-state automata, the semantics of 1-counter automata are given by a set
A
of accepting states and an initial configuration
(q_{0},n_{0})
. A finite word
w
is accepted if there is an accepting state
q_{f}
and a natural number
n_{f}
for which
(q_{0},n_{0})\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q_{f},n_{f})
.

We should remark that our model is phrased in a slightly different manner from other formulations
[
46
]
that describe one-counter automata as pushdown automata with a single stack symbol, allow for increments of more than one unit in a single step, etc. Our formulation makes the parallel between types and automata somewhat more evident, and simplifies some of the proofs. It should be clear that our formulation is equivalent to the standard formulations, i.e., one can easily convert between them.

We now explain how to convert 1-counter session types into 1-counter automata. Instead of non-parameterised variables our equations now involve terms of the
form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, etc. We assume for simplicity that the variables appearing
in these equations are restricted in the following way: if the left-hand side of
an equation is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, then the variables appearing in the
right-hand side must be of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
(with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}
possibly different from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
); and
if the left-hand side of an equation is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, then
the variables appearing in the right-hand side must be of the form

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. Any
system can be converted into this form by adding finitely many new equations.
For example,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
can be
rewritten as

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}.

We can convert a 1-counter type into a (deterministic) 1-counter automaton, so
that the transition function depends on whether the counter value is zero
(corresponding to a right-hand side of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
) or positive
(corresponding to a right-hand side of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
).
Furthermore, the changes in the counter value along the variables are
incorporated by changes in the counter value along the automaton. For example,
take equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}
. The corresponding
transition from
(q_{X},\mathsf{s},\varepsilon)
to
q_{Y}
decrements the counter.
