The corresponding automaton has states
q_{X},q_{Y},q_{Z}
, one for for each type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}
, as well as an additional state

q_{\mathsf{end}}
. The outgoing transitions for state
q_{X}
are the same regardless of
the counter value: either read
\&\mathrm{inc}
, incrementing the counter and staying
in
q_{X}
; or read
\&\mathrm{dump}
, keeping the counter value and moving to
q_{Y}
.
For state
q_{Y}
, if the counter is zero, we can read
\mathsf{end}
while moving to
state
q_{\mathsf{end}}
. On the other hand, if the counter is non-zero, we can read

!\mathsf{d}
, keeping the counter value and moving to
q_{Z}
; or read
!\mathsf{c}
,
decrementing the counter value and staying in
q_{Y}
. Finally, for state
q_{Z}

we can only read
\mathsf{end}
and move to state
q_{\mathsf{end}}
. Note that whatever we
choose to write on the equation for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is irrelevant, as this
configuration is unreachable. Putting all these together, we arrive at the
automaton in
Fig.

11
.

Pushdown types and automata

Just as the notion of 1-counter automata allows us to define a new class of types that extends the regular types, we can use (deterministic) pushdown automata to obtain the next class in our hierarchy of types. The main difference between pushdown automata and 1-counter automata is the ability of using a stack of symbols over a finite stack alphabet instead of a counter (which can be thought of as a stack over a singleton alphabet).

We use
\Delta
to denote a finite
stack alphabet
. The contents of a
stack are denoted by a word
\omega\in\Delta^{\ast}
, with
\varepsilon

representing an empty stack. We follow the convention that the first (leftmost)
symbol in
\omega
corresponds to the top symbol of the stack. For ease of
notation, let
\mathrm{Op}=\{+\sigma:\sigma\in\Delta\}\cup\{=,-\}
denote
the different stack operations (push a symbol
\sigma
onto the stack, do
nothing, or pop the stack). In a (deterministic) pushdown automaton, we have a
partial-valued transition function

\delta:Q\times(\{\varepsilon\}\cup\Delta)\times(\{\varepsilon\}\cup\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{Op}\times Q
. The transition function takes as input the current state, the current top
symbol of the stack (or an indication that the stack is empty), and the next
character of the word to be read (or an indication of an
\varepsilon
-move).
The output of the transition function is composed of a stack operation and the
next state.
7
7

7

Similarly to 1-counter automata, we can syntatically
exclude the possibility that
\delta
outputs a pop operation when the stack
is empty by building automata from well formed pushdown types.
We are solely interested in
deterministic, total
transitions, which mean that at each combination
(q,\sigma)\in Q\times(\{\varepsilon\}\cup\Delta)
we can either only perform an
\varepsilon
-move (
\delta(q,\sigma,a)
is undefined for all
a\in\Sigma
), or only perform reading moves (
\delta(q,\sigma,\varepsilon)
is undefined). A configuration is given by a pair
(q,\omega)\in Q\times\Delta^{\ast}
, where
q
denotes the current state and
\omega
the current contents of the stack. In a similar way to 1-counter automata, we can define the notion of a derivation
(q,\omega)\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
as a sequence of moves going from
(q,\omega)
to
(q^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
while reading the word
w
over the input symbols. Again, we observe that several configurations may be derived from the same input word
w
, and that they belong in a unique path of
\varepsilon
-moves.

Finally, the semantics of a pushdown automata is given by a set
A\subseteq Q
of accepting states, and an initial configuration
(q_{0},\omega_{0})
. A word
w
is accepted if there is an accepting state
q_{f}
and a stack word
\omega_{f}
for which
(q_{0},\omega_{0})\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q_{f},\omega_{f})
. A
deterministic context-free language
(DCFL) is a language accepted by a deterministic pushdown automaton.

Again, we should remark the ways in which our formulation differs from the
standard
[
23
]
: we allow the transition function to
be defined on an empty stack, but we forbid pushing multiple stack symbols in a single transition. However, one can easily convert between formulations by adding extra symbols and states.

Pushdown systems act in a similar manner, but now the behaviour of a variable is specified by
|\Delta|+1
equations, where
\Delta
is the stack alphabet; one equation for each possible symbol at the top of the stack, and one equation for the case that the stack is empty. Accordingly, we use a (deterministic) pushdown automaton to simulate the stack contents by means of push and pop operations. The transitions from a state
q_{X}
and a given stack indicator in
\{\varepsilon\}\cup\Delta
are once more given by the corresponding equation with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
as the type constructor on the left-hand side.
Fig.

12
shows a pushdown automaton
accepting
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}})
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{pop}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau S}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma\tau S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau\tau S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{pop}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\}}}

2-counter types and automata

For the final step in our hierarchy we could think of extending the number of counters, or the number of stacks, of the representation models presented above. It should be clear by now that we would thus establish a correspondence from a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
having a representation in terms of, say,
k
counters, to a (deterministic) automaton with
k
auxiliary counters accepting the language
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
. However, we know that this hierarchy collapses after
k=2
in the Turing machine model
[
23
, Chapter 7]
. That is, any language that is decidable (in the usual sense of the word) is accepted by a 2-counter automaton (and hence, also by a 2-stack pushdown automaton).

The translation to 2-counter automata is as for the 1-counter case, but now the
behaviour is specified by one of four different cases, depending on which of the
two counters is zero or non-zero. Accordingly, we use a (deterministic)
2-counter automaton with the appropriate transition function.

5
From automata to types

The constructions in
Section

4
explain how, given a system of equations at some level in the hierarchy, we can construct a corresponding automaton. If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma}\rangle}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{p}}}}
, then the language of the type given by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the language accepted by the automaton with initial configuration
(q_{X},\sigma)
(and similarly for recursive, 1-counter, and 2-counter types).
Conversely, given an automaton which is promised to accept the language of traces of a type, we can construct the corresponding system of equations that specifies that type. This allow us to obtain a complete correspondence between classes of types and different models of computation based on automata theory.

Let us begin with the following observation. From
Proposition

1
, we know that the language of a type is prefix-closed. Furthermore, the construction in
Section

4
gives rise to automata with the following interesting property: they have exactly one non-accepting state (
q_{\mathrm{error}}
), from which one cannot escape (all transitions from
q_{\mathrm{error}}
lead to
q_{\mathrm{error}}
). It should be obvious that automata with such a property accept prefix-closed languages.

Definition 1
.

An automaton is said to be obviously prefix-closed if it has exactly one non-accepting state, and this state is a sink.

If our given automaton is obviously prefix-closed, and accepts the language of a type, it is straightforward to retrieve from its description the equivalent system of equations (as we shall see in this section, after
Theorem

4
). However, what if the given automaton is not obviously prefix-closed, but it still promised to accept a prefix-closed language? We answer this question by showing how to convert such an automaton into an equivalent automaton which is obviously prefix-closed. For the case of finite-state automata the proof is straightforward (see for example Kao et al.
[
25
]
); for the remaining three classes of automata, this is (to the best of our knowledge) a novel contribution of our paper.

As a first stage in our construction, we convert a given automaton into an equivalent automaton in the following form.

Definition 2
.

An automaton (with initial configuration
c_{0}
) is said to be in normal form if it satisfies the following two properties.

•

guaranteed to read: for any input word
w
, there exists some configuration
c^{\prime}
for which
c_{0}\overset{w}{\rightarrow}c^{\prime}
;

•

immediate acceptance: for any input word
w
, let
c^{\prime}
be the first configuration for which
c_{0}\overset{w}{\rightarrow}c^{\prime}
. Then
w
is accepted by the automaton iff
c^{\prime}
is an accepting configuration.

The first property (guaranteed to read) intuitively means that
the automaton cannot get stuck in an infinite sequence of
\varepsilon
-moves. Immediate acceptance means that we can decide whether an input word
w
is accepted by the automaton immediately after reading its last symbol.

Of course, many different “normal forms” of automata have been adopted in the
literature. One which is particularly close to ours appears in
Valiant’s PhD thesis
[
45
]
; in his normal form (for 1-counter and pushdown
automata), the decision of acceptance is postponed until the last (as opposed to
the first) configuration
c^{\prime}
for which
c_{0}\overset{w}{\rightarrow}c^{\prime}

(implying, in other words, that all accepting states must correspond to reading
modes). A consequence of the following result is that these are all equivalent
automata formulations.

Theorem 3

(Normal form automata)
.

•

Any finite-state automaton can be converted into an equivalent normal form automaton.

•

Any 1-counter automaton can be converted into an equivalent normal form automaton.

•

Any pushdown automaton can be converted into an equivalent normal form automaton.

•

Any decidable language is accepted by a 2-counter normal form automaton.

Proof.

In
Appendix

B
.
∎

With the above characterisation, we are now able to prove that prefix-closed languages can be assumed to be accepted by obviously prefix-closed automata.

Theorem 4
.

•

Every prefix-closed regular language is accepted by an obviously prefix-closed finite-state automaton.

•

Every prefix-closed language accepted by a one-counter automaton is accepted by an obviously prefix-closed one-counter automaton.

•

Every prefix-closed DCFL is accepted by an obviously prefix-closed pushdown automaton.

•

Every prefix-closed decidable language is accepted by an obviously prefix-closed two-counter automaton.

Proof.

The proof is identical in all four cases. Let
L
be a language fitting into one of the above four cases, and without loss assume
L\neq\emptyset
. Let
A
be an automaton accepting
L
. Due to
Theorem

3
,
A
can be assumed to be in normal form. We now construct an automaton
A^{\prime}
by modifying
A
as follows.

•

A^{\prime}
has a fresh, non-accepting state
q_{\mathrm{error}}
; every configuration associated with
q_{\mathrm{error}}
is a reading configuration for which reading
a
moves again to
q_{\mathrm{error}}
, for every input symbol
a
;

•

Let
c\overset{a}{\rightarrow}c^{\prime}
be a reading move in
A
, and
q^{\prime}
the state corresponding to
c^{\prime}
. If
q^{\prime}
was not an accepting state for
A
, then replace this transition by a reading move
c\overset{a}{\rightarrow}c^{\prime}_{\mathrm{error}}
, where
c^{\prime}_{\mathrm{error}}
is like
c^{\prime}
except the corresponding state is
q_{\mathrm{error}}
instead of
q^{\prime}
.

•

Make every state in
A
accepting in
A^{\prime}
(so that
q_{\mathrm{error}}
becomes the unique non-accepting state).

By construction,
A^{\prime}
is obviously prefix-closed. It remains to show that it accepts the same language
L
. Let
w
be an input word in
L
. Since
L
is prefix-closed, each of the prefixes of
w
is in
L
. Since
A
is in normal form, each of the reading moves in the computation of
A
on
w
lead to an accepting state. Therefore, the computation of
A^{\prime}
on
w
simulates the same transitions as those of
A
. In particular, it never transitions to state
q_{\mathrm{error}}
. Thus,
A^{\prime}
accepts
w
.

Now suppose that
w
is an input word not in
L
. Decompose
w
as
w^{\prime}aw^{\prime\prime}
, where
w^{\prime}
is the largest prefix of
w
such that
w^{\prime}\in L
. This largest prefix exists since
L
is prefix-closed and non-empty (in particular, the empty word must belong to
L
). By the previous argument, the computation of
A^{\prime}
in
w^{\prime}
simulates the same transitions as those of
A
. Let
c\overset{a}{\rightarrow}c^{\prime}
be the reading move that reads
a
in the computation of
A
. Since
A
is in normal form and
w^{\prime}a\not\in L
, the state
q^{\prime}
corresponding to
c^{\prime}
is a non-accepting state of
A
. Therefore, the computation of
A^{\prime}
for
w
transitions at this point to the state
q_{\mathrm{error}}
, and remains there for the rest of the computation. Thus,
A^{\prime}
rejects
w
. This concludes our proof.
∎

The final ingredient before proving
Theorem

5
is an explanation on how to construct a system of equations, given an obviously prefix-closed automaton accepting
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
, for some type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. Here we sketch only the construction for pushdown automata, as the ideas are essentially the same for the other models. For each accepting state
q
, we have a corresponding variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{q}}}
. For each mode
(q,\varepsilon)
(resp.
(q,\sigma)
), we define the right-hand side corresponding to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{q}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{q}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
) according to the following case analysis (we sketch the case
(q,\varepsilon)
, as the analysis for
(q,\sigma)
is identical):

•

Suppose
(q,\varepsilon)
is an
\varepsilon
-mode, with corresponding transition to, say,
(+\sigma,q^{\prime})
. Then our system contains the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{q}}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{q^{\prime}}}\langle{\sigma}\rangle}}}
.

•

Suppose
(q,\varepsilon)
is a reading mode, and that reading
\mathsf{end}
has a transition to an accepting state, say,
(=,q^{\prime})
. Then, for any word
w
such that there is a sequence of moves
(q_{0},\omega_{0})\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q,\varepsilon)
,
w\cdot\mathsf{end}
is a word in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
. By the way
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is defined, it must be the only such word having
w
as a proper prefix. Therefore, if such a word
w
exists, reading any other symbol from configuration
(q,\varepsilon)
must cause the automaton to transition to the non-accepting state. We include the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{q}}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
in our system.

•

Suppose
(q,\varepsilon)
is a reading mode, and that reading
?\mathsf{d}
has a transition to an accepting state, say,
(=,q^{\prime})
. Then, for any word
w
such that
(q_{0},\omega_{0})\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q,\varepsilon)
,
w\cdot?\mathsf{d}
is a word in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
. This word and
w\cdot?\mathsf{c}
must be the only two words in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
that are immediate continuations of
w
. Therefore, if such a word
w
exists, reading any other symbol must cause the automaton to transition to the non-accepting state. Suppose the state reached from
(q,\varepsilon)
after reading
?\mathsf{c}
is, say,
(+\sigma,q^{\prime\prime})
. We include the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{q}}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{{X_{q^{\prime}}}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle}}}.{{X_{q^{\prime\prime}}}\langle{\sigma}\rangle}}}}}}
in our system.

•

A similar analysis takes care of the other cases in which reading a symbol leads to an accepting state. Notice that it is technically possible for multiple contradictory symbols to have reading moves to accepting states. For instance, it could be the case that
(q,\varepsilon)\overset{\mathsf{end}}{\rightarrow}(=,q^{\prime})
and
(q,\varepsilon)\overset{!\mathsf{c}}{\rightarrow}(=,q^{\prime\prime})
, with both
q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}
accepting. However, by the way
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is defined, this only occurs if there is no word
w
such that
(q_{0},\omega_{0})\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q,\varepsilon)
. Thus, we can put either option in the right-hand of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, as this type constructor will also not be reachable.

•

The only case left is if
(q,\varepsilon)
is a reading mode, but reading any symbol leads to the non-accepting state. This means that, for any
w
with
(q_{0},\omega_{0})\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q,\varepsilon)
, there is no other word in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
having
w
as a prefix. By the way
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is defined,
w
must end with the symbol
\mathsf{end}
. This again means that the right-hand side of equation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is irrelevant, as this variable will also not be reachable. We can define the corresponding equation to be
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
by default.

With the construction outlined above, we are able to prove the main result of this section. The following is a characterisation result that establishes a correspondence between classes of types and
different models of computation based on automata theory. We remark that our result is stronger than previous similar results which only show a forward implication
[
10
]
.
Recall that a language is said to be
regular
if it is the set of words accepted by some finite-state automaton. We also say that a tree is
regular
if it has a finite number of distinct subtrees.

Theorem 5

(Types, traces and automata)
.

1.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{r}}}}
iff
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is regular iff
\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is regular.

2.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{1}}}}
iff
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is accepted by a 1-counter automaton.

3.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{p}}}}
iff
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is a deterministic context-free language.

4.

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{2}}}}
iff
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is decidable.

Proof.

The proof is identical for each of the four cases. In the forward direction,
consider a system of equations that specify a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
in one of the four
classes, and use the construction in
Section

4
to obtain
the corresponding automata that accepts
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
. In the reverse
direction, suppose that the language
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is in one of the four models
of computation. Using
Theorem

4
, we know that an
obviously prefix-closed automaton exists that accepts
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
. Using the
construction preceding this theorem, we can obtain the
corresponding system of equations that specifies
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
.
The only case left is to prove that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{r}}}}
iff
\operatorname{treeof}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
is regular.
However, for recursive types this has been observed before by Pierce
[
35
, Chapter
21]
.
∎

6
The hierarchy of type classes

Using the above characterisation, we can show that the hierarchy of types is strict
((
2
);
Theorem

2
). The main idea in proving that our various formalisms for session types have different expressive power is to leverage known separation techniques from formal language theory, such as the pumping lemma. We illustrate the technique with the separation
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{1}}
.

Lemma 1
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a recursive type, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a 1-counter type. On the other hand,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}}
is a 1-counter type but not a recursive type.

Proof.

Clearly, a system of recursive equations describing a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
can be converted into a system of 1-counter equations whose transitions do not depend on the counter value, thus describing the same type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. On the other hand, suppose, for the sake of deriving a contradiction, that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}}
was a recursive type. By
Theorem

5
, we would conclude that its language
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}})
is regular. Next, we apply the pumping lemma for regular languages
[
23
, Section 3.1]
: there must be a constant
n
such that any word
z\in\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}})
with
|z|\geq n
can be written as
z=uvw
with
|uv|\leq n
,
|v|\geq 1
, and
uv^{i}w\in\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}})
for every
i\geq 0
. Take

z=\left(\&\mathrm{inc}\right)^{n}\cdot\&\mathrm{dump}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{n}\cdot\mathsf{end}

It is clear that
z
fits the condition in the pumping lemma, and that any
v
in the desired decomposition must be a substring of
\left(\&\mathrm{inc}\right)^{n}
. However,
z
is the only word in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{counter}}}})
having
\&\mathrm{dump}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{n}\cdot\mathsf{end}
as a suffix, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

To prove the separation between 1-counter types and pushdown types, we need to use a variant of the pumping lemma for 1-counter automata. The following result is due to Boasson.

Lemma 2

(Boasson
[
7
, Theorem 3]
)
.

Let
L
be a language accepted by a 1-counter automaton. Suppose that
f
is a word in
L
having a decomposition

f=g_{1}ug_{2}vg_{3}xg_{4}yg_{5}

with the following properties:

1.

u,v,x,y
are non-empty words;

2.

for all
n,m\geq 0
, the word
g_{1}u^{n}g_{2}v^{m}g_{3}x^{m}g_{4}y^{n}g_{5}
is in
L
;

3.

for all
n\geq 0
, the set

\displaystyle\{m\,:\,

\displaystyle g_{1}u^{n}g_{2}vg_{3}xg_{4}y^{m}g_{5}\in L\text{ or }g_{1}u^{m}g_{2}vg_{3}xg_{4}y^{n}g_{5}\in L

or

\displaystyle g_{1}ug_{2}v^{n}g_{3}x^{m}g_{4}yg_{5}\in L\text{ or }g_{1}ug_{2}v^{m}g_{3}x^{n}g_{4}yg_{5}\in L\}

is finite.

Then, there exist
n,m,\lambda,\mu\geq 1
such that for all
k\geq 0
, the word

g_{1}u^{n+\lambda k-1}g_{2}vg_{3}x^{m+\mu k-1}g_{4}yg_{5}\in L.

Lemma 3
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a 1-counter type, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a pushdown type. On the other hand,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}}
is a pushdown type but not a 1-counter type.

Proof.

Clearly, a system of 1-counter equations describing a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
can be converted into a system of 1-stack equations, whose stack has a unique symbol, and where the value of the counter corresponds to the size of the stack.

On the other hand, suppose, for the sake of deriving a contradiction, that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}}
was a 1-counter type. By
Theorem

5
, we would conclude that its language
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}})
is accepted by a 1-counter automata. Now consider the following family of words parameterized by
n,n^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}\geq 0
:

f_{n,n^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}}=\&\mathrm{addIn}\cdot(\&\mathrm{addOut})^{n+1}\cdot(\&\mathrm{addIn})^{m}\cdot(\&\mathrm{pop}\cdot?\mathsf{c})^{m^{\prime}}\cdot(\&\mathrm{pop}\cdot!\mathsf{c})^{n^{\prime}+1}\cdot\&\mathrm{pop}\cdot?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}

Intuitively,
f_{n,n^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}}
corresponds to the following sequence of interactions: pushing the symbol
\tau
; pushing
n+1
copies of the symbol
\sigma
; pushing
m
copies of the symbol
\tau
; popping the top symbol
\tau
from the stack
m^{\prime}
times; popping the top symbol
\sigma
from the stack
n^{\prime}+1
times; and popping the top symbol
\tau
from the stack. By our construction of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}}
, it should be clear that
f_{n,n^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}})
iff
n=n^{\prime}
and
m=m^{\prime}
(the reason for pushing each symbol
\sigma
,
\tau
at least once is to exclude situations where the number of times a symbol is pushed would be higher than the number of times that symbol is popped).

In particular, the word
f_{1,1,1,1}
satisfies the conditions in
Lemma

2
with the decomposition

g_{1}=\&\mathrm{addIn},\quad g_{2}=\&\mathrm{addOut},\quad g_{3}=\varepsilon,\quad g_{4}=\&\mathrm{pop}\cdot!\mathsf{c},\quad g_{5}=\&\mathrm{pop}\cdot?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end},

u=\&\mathrm{addOut},\quad v=\&\mathrm{addIn},\quad x=\&\mathrm{pop}\cdot?\mathsf{c},\quad y=\&\mathrm{pop}\cdot!\mathsf{c}.

Applying that lemma, we would conclude that there exist
n,m,\lambda,\mu\geq 1
such that, for all
k\geq 0
, the word
f_{n+\lambda k-1,1,m+\mu k-1,1}
is in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}})
. However, from our previous discussion, this means that
n+\lambda k-1=1
and
m+\mu k-1=1
, which cannot be true for all
k\geq 0
. We have thus derived our contradicion.
∎

Lemma 4
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a pushdown type, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is a 2-counter type. On the other hand,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}}
is a 2-counter type but not a pushdown type.

Proof.

The inclusion follows from
Theorem

5
and the observation that all DCFLs are decidable. On the other hand, suppose, for the sake of deriving a contradiction, that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}}
was a pushdown type. By
Theorem

5
, we would conclude that its language
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
is a DCFL, and in particular, a context-free language. Next, we apply the pumping lemma for context-free languages (Section 6.1 in Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
]
): there must be a constant
n
such that any word
z\in\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
with
|z|\geq n
can be written as
z=uvwxy
with
|vwx|\leq n
,
|vx|\geq 1
, and
uv^{i}wx^{i}y\in\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
for every
i\geq 0
. Consider the following sequence of words in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
, for
k\geq 0
:

z_{k}=?\mathsf{c}\cdot!\mathsf{c}\cdot?\mathsf{c}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{2}\cdots?\mathsf{c}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{k}\cdot?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}

From inspection, we can conclude that
z_{k}
are the only words in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
that end in
?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}
, and that in
z_{k}
the character
?\mathsf{c}
appears exactly
k
times, the character
!\mathsf{c}
appears exactly
1+\ldots+k=\frac{k(k+1)}{2}
times, and the characters
?\mathsf{d}
and
\mathsf{end}
appear exactly once. Now apply the pumping lemma to get a decomposition of
z_{n}=uvwxy
, and consider the following two cases:

•

Suppose
vwx
is contained in the prefix
?\mathsf{c}\cdot!\mathsf{c}\cdots?\mathsf{c}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{n-1}
of
z_{n}
. In this case,
y
contains
?\mathsf{c}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{n}\cdot?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}
as a suffix, and thus so do
uv^{i}wx^{i}y
for any
i
. On the other hand,
z_{n}
is the only word in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
having
?\mathsf{c}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{n}\cdot?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}
as a suffix, which results in a contradiction.

•

Suppose now that
vwx
intersects the suffix
?\mathsf{c}\cdot(!\mathsf{c})^{n}\cdot?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}
of
z_{k}
. Since every word in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
contains at most one
?\mathsf{d}
and one
\mathsf{end}
,
v
and
x
cannot contain those characters. Since
|vwx|\leq n
, the character
?\mathsf{c}
can appear at most once in
vwx
. If
?\mathsf{c}
never appears in
vx
, then
uwy
is a word ending in
?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}
with
n
occurences of the character
?\mathsf{c}
and strictly fewer than
1+\ldots+n
occurences of the character
!\mathsf{c}
. If
?\mathsf{c}
appears once in
vx
, then
!\mathsf{c}
appears at most
n-1
times in
vx
. In this case,
uwy
is a word ending in
?\mathsf{d}\cdot\mathsf{end}
with
n-1
occurences of the character
?\mathsf{c}
and strictly more than
1+\ldots+(n-1)
occurences of the character
!c
. In either case,
uwy
cannot be one of the words
z_{k}
and thus cannot be in
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{iter}}}})
, from which we derive our contradiction.

∎

At the end of the hierarchy, we can prove the separation
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{2}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{\infty}}
by a cardinality argument.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
be the set of types that can be represented by some finite
representation system. Then
\mathbb{T}^{\prime}
is a strict subset of
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{\infty}}
. In particular,
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{2}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{\infty}}
.

Proof.

Notice that the set of all possible infinite types is uncountable. In particular, for every infinite word
w=b_{0}b_{1}b_{2}\ldots
over the alphabet
\{0,1\}
, we can define the type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{w}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp_{0}\mathsf{end}.\sharp_{1}\mathsf{end}.\sharp_{2}\mathsf{end}.\ldots}}

where
\sharp_{n}
is either
?
if
b_{n}=0
or
!
if
b_{n}=1
. As the set of such infinite words is uncountable, and
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{w}}})\neq\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{w^{\prime}}}})
for
w\neq w^{\prime}
, so is the set of all types. Moreover, any finite representation system can contain at most a countable set of types. Hence, we get the desired result.
∎

Notice that the cardinality argument presented above also shows that there is in fact an
infinite, uncountable
set of types that cannot be represented by a given finite representation system.

7
Results for context-free and nested session types

Here we compare the context-free session types model
[
1
,
2
]
and
the nested session types model
[
10
]
with the main hierarchy of our paper; i.e., we prove the inclusions in (
3
) and (
4
), which complete the proof of
Theorem

2
.

Regarding context-free session types, it is quite clear that they extend recursive types: a recursive system of equations can be converted into the context-free syntax by replacing
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
with
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T}}}.{U}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T}}}};{U}}}
. The following result shows that
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}
.

Theorem 6
.

Let
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
, and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
be such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. Then, there exists a representation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
as a pushdown type, having the following properties:

•

The representation uses a single type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
.

•

The only occurrence of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
is in the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
.

Proof.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
be a context-free session type represented by some system of equations.
Without loss of generality, assume that this system is in the following normal form: in all equations
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{i}}}}
, the
right-hand side
T_{i}
is given by only one type construct. We construct a
pushdown type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
using a single variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
. For each type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}
in
the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, we have a corresponding stack symbol
\sigma_{i}
. Finally,
we translate the equations defining
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
into equations defining
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, as
follows.

•

For each equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}
, we have an equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i}S}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{S}\rangle}}}
.

•

For each equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{X}}}_{i^{\prime}}}}}
, we have an equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i}S}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i^{\prime}}}\rangle}}}.{{X}\langle{S}\rangle}}}}}}
.

•

For each equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{l_{j}}\colon{X_{i_{j}}}\}_{{l_{j}}\in{L}}}}}
, we have an equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i}S}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{l_{j}}\colon{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i_{j}}S}\rangle}\}_{{l_{j}}\in{L}}}}}
.

•

For each equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i_{1}};X_{i_{2}}}}}
, we have an equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i}S}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}S}\rangle}}}
.

•

For each equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i^{\prime}}}}}
, we have an equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i}S}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\sigma_{i^{\prime}}S}\rangle}}}
.

•

Additionally, we have the equation
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
.

A simple coinductive proof then shows that, if the context-free session type is
given by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is a pushdown representation of the type corresponding to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
.
∎

In fact, the previous result shows something stronger than
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}
: it shows that
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}
is a subset of the first level
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}
of the hierarchy within pushdown session types (defined in
Section

3
).

Next, we argue that context-free and 1-counter types are incomparable, which implies the strict inclusions
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}\subsetneq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}
. In our separation of
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}
from
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{1}}
(
Lemma

3
), we show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{meta}}}}
is not a 1-counter type. However, we can represent it as a context-free type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\varepsilon}}}
with

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\varepsilon}}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{\sigma}};{X_{\varepsilon}}}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{\tau}};{X_{\varepsilon}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\sigma}}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{\sigma}};{X_{\sigma}}}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{\tau}};{X_{\sigma}}}},\mathrm{pop}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\tau}}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{addOut}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{\sigma}};{X_{\tau}}}},\mathrm{addIn}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{\tau}};{X_{\tau}}}},\mathrm{pop}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}\}}}

On the other-hand, from the work of Korenjak and Hopcroft
[
27
]
we know that the language
L_{3}=\{\mathrm{l}^{n}\;\mathrm{a}\;\mathrm{r}^{n}\;\mathrm{a}\mid n\geq 0\}\cup\{\mathrm{l}^{n}\;\mathrm{b}\;\mathrm{r}^{n}\;\mathrm{b}\mid n\geq 0\}
is deterministic context-free but cannot be accepted by a DPDA with a single state. This was used by Das et al.
[
10
]
to argue that context-free session types cannot express language
L_{3}
. However, we can use 1-counter types to express this language, i.e., define the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{KH}}}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
with equations

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{l}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{a}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{b}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{l}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{a}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\mathrm{b}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{a}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{r}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{z}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{b}\colon\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{s}}}\,N}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq

\displaystyle\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathrm{r}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{N}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\}}}

We conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{KH}}}}
is a 1-counter type but not a context-free type.

Next we look at the equivalence between pushdown and nested session types.
Recall that
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{n}
corresponds to the types that have pushdown representations with at most
n
type constructors, whereas
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{n}
corresponds to the types that have nested representations with type constructors of arity at most
n
. The following result shows that
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{n}\subseteq\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{n}
.

Theorem 7
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
be a pushdown session type with at most
n
type constructors. Then, there is a nested session type representation for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, using type constructors of arity at most
n
.

Proof.

Consider a pushdown representation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
using the type constructors
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(n)}}}
and a stack alphabet
\Delta
. We consider a nested session type representation using

•

for each variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}
, a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}_{\varepsilon}}}
of arity
0
;

•

for each variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}
and each stack symbol
\sigma
, a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}_{\sigma}}}
of arity
n
.

We translate each equation in the pushdown representation into an equation for the corresponding type constructor. We use the
n
variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{n}}}
in our equations. The translation only needs to convert type variables into type constructors:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}_{\varepsilon}}}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}_{\sigma}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}_{\varepsilon},\ldots,X^{(n)}_{\varepsilon}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}_{\sigma}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{i}}}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}_{\sigma}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(1)}_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\rangle,\ldots,{X^{(n)}_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
.

Intuitively, each type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}_{\sigma}}}
corresponds to the stage where variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}
needs to be unfolded with
\sigma
at the top of the stack. The arguments stored during the unfolding keep track of all possible ways one can empty the current stack. The argument at position
i
is chosen if the pushdown unfolding would move to variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}
. A simple coinductive proof shows that both representations yield the same type.
∎

We illustrate the above proof with an example. Consider the pushdown session
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
with

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathsf{pushOut}:{X}\langle{\sigma}\rangle,\mathsf{pushIn}:{X}\langle{\tau}\rangle,\mathsf{dump}:{Y}\langle{\varepsilon}\rangle\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathsf{pushOut}:{X}\langle{\sigma\sigma S}\rangle,\mathsf{pushIn}:{X}\langle{\tau\sigma S}\rangle,\mathsf{pop}:{X}\langle{S}\rangle,\mathsf{dump}:{Y}\langle{\sigma S}\rangle\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathsf{pushOut}:{X}\langle{\sigma\tau S}\rangle,\mathsf{pushIn}:{X}\langle{\tau\tau S}\rangle,\mathsf{pop}:{X}\langle{S}\rangle,\mathsf{dump}:{Y}\langle{\tau S}\rangle\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}.{Y}\langle{S}\rangle}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tau S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}.{Y}\langle{S}\rangle}}

The above session type is a variant of
Example

4
with two type
variables. It offers a choice of pushing either symbol
\sigma
or
\tau
into
the stack, popping the stack, or dumping the entire stack contents. When
dumping, the value at the top of the stack (
\sigma
or
\tau
) determines
whether an
!\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
or
?\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
message is triggered. Applying the conversion in
the proof of the previous theorem, we get the following representation of the
session type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\varepsilon}}}
, using constructors of arity at most 2, which can be seen to yield the same type.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathsf{pushOut}:{X_{\sigma}}\langle{X_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}}\rangle,\mathsf{pushIn}:{X_{\tau}}\langle{X_{\varepsilon},Y_{\varepsilon}}\rangle,\mathsf{dump}:Y_{\varepsilon}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\sigma}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathsf{pushOut}:{X_{\sigma}}\langle{{X_{\sigma}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{Y_{\sigma}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle}\rangle,}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\qquad\mathsf{pushIn}:{X_{\tau}}\langle{{X_{\sigma}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{Y_{\sigma}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle}\rangle,}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\qquad\mathsf{pop}:\alpha_{1},\mathsf{dump}:\alpha_{2}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{\tau}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\&\{\mathsf{pushOut}:{X_{\sigma}}\langle{{X_{\tau}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{Y_{\tau}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle}\rangle,}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\qquad\mathsf{pushIn}:{X_{\tau}}\langle{{X_{\tau}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{Y_{\tau}}\langle{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\rangle}\rangle,}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\qquad\mathsf{pop}:\alpha_{1},\mathsf{dump}:\alpha_{2}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y_{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\doteq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y_{\sigma}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{!\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}.\alpha_{2}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y_{\tau}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}.\alpha_{2}}}

The remainder of this section is devoted to the reverse implication, i.e., that nested session types can be simulated with pushdown session types. As a warmup, we begin by looking at nested session types using unary constructors. The following result shows that
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{1}
is contained in
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}
.

Lemma 6
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
be a nested session type using type constructors of arity at most one. Then, there is a pushdown session type representation for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
using only one type constructor.

Proof.

Consider a nested session type representation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
using type constructors
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(n)}}}
. By renaming the variables, we can assume that the single argument of every type constructor is denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}
.

We convert the nested session type representation into a pushdown session type representation as follows. We have a single type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
; for each type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}
, we have a corresponding stack symbol
\sigma_{i}
. The equation in the nested session type representation corresponding to type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}
is converted into the equation for the case that
\sigma_{i}
is at the top of the stack. Namely, the translation converts nested type constructors into type variables:

•

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i_{k})}}}
has arity
0
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i_{1})}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots{}\langle{X^{(i_{k})}}\rangle\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i_{1}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{k}}S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
;

•

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i_{k})}}}
has arity
1
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i_{1})}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots{}\langle{{X^{(i_{k})}}\langle{\alpha}\rangle}\rangle\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i_{1}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{k}}S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
;

Finally, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i_{1})}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\ldots{}\langle{X^{(i_{k})}}\rangle\ldots}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the
initial type on the nested session type representation, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i_{1}}\ldots\sigma_{i_{k}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the corresponding initial type in the pushdown session type representation. A simple coinductive proof shows that both representations yield the same type.
∎

With significant more effort, we can extend the above simulation to
n
-ary constructors.

Theorem 8
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
be a nested session type using type constructors of arity at most
n
. Then, there is a pushdown session type representation for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
using only
n
type variables.

Proof.

In
Appendix

C
.
∎

Given the close relationship between pushdown and nested session types, we make at this point some important remarks comparing both models.

•

The proofs in this section also provide algorithms for converting between (representations of) pushdown session types and nested session types. It can be seen that both algorithms run in polynomial time, and in particular they incur only a polynomial overhead. In other words, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has a pushdown representation of size
n
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
has a nested representation of size at most
\mathrm{poly}(n)
and vice-versa.

•

We arrived at our hierachy of session types by thinking about equational definitions and about possible ways by which the type constructors can be parameterized. This makes pushdown session types a ‘natural’ level of the hierarchy, with 1-counter and 2-counter types as other natural choices. Nested session types, however, arised by thinking of type constructors that are applied to other type constructors. It is not obvious what would be the counterparts of 1-counter or 2-counter session types in the nested session type framework.

•

As we unfold a pushdown session type, its encoding size can only grow polynomially, whereas the unfolding of a nested session type can grow exponentially on the number of steps. More formally, suppose we sequentially unfold a pushdown expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
: at each stage, we choose a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
appearing in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and replace it according to the appropriate equation. Then, the expression achieved at stage
n
of this unfolding has size bounded by
\mathrm{poly}(n)
. On the other hand, consider a nested representation of the type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\mathrm{loop}}}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y,Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
with equations

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\alpha,\beta}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{?{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}.{{X}\langle{{X}\langle{\alpha,\beta}\rangle,{X}\langle{\alpha,\beta}\rangle}\rangle}}}}}}

one can see that at each unfolding step the encoding size (e.g. the number of characters) of the nested session type doubles and so after
n
steps we reach an expression of size
\varTheta(2^{n})
. Hence pushdown types permit a more efficient direct representation of their unfoldings.

8
Decidability/Undecidability of Key Problems

We are now in a position to address the decidability of the key problems of type
formation, type equivalence and type duality for the various classes of type
languages studied in this paper.

Before looking at type formation, we need to study the problem of deciding type contractiveness, described in
Figs.

2
,
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
. Let us say that a system of recursive equations over
\mathcal{X}
is contractive if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{X}
. Similarly, a system of 1-counter equations over
\mathcal{X}
is said to be contractive if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{n}\rangle}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
for every
X\in\mathcal{X}
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We can extend this notion in the obvious way to pushdown systems and 2-counter systems.

From the construction described in
Section

4
, we can inherit contractiveness conditions by looking at loop-freeness of the associated automata. The following definition captures the notion of loop-freeness (more precisely,
\varepsilon
-loop-freeness) for all automata models (see also Ginsburg and Greibach
[
18
]
and Valiant
[
45
]
). By a configuration we mean: in finite-state automata, a state
q\in Q
; in 1-counter automata, a pair
(q,n)\in Q\times\mathbb{N}
; in pushdown automata, a pair
(q,\omega)\in Q\times\Delta^{\ast}
; and in 2-counter automata, a triple
(q,n,m)\in Q\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 3
.

An automaton is said to be loop-free if, for every configuration
c
, the sequence of
\varepsilon
-moves started from
c
eventually reaches a reading configuration.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\mathrm{Sys}
be a system of recursive equations (resp. 1-counter equations, pushdown equations, 2-counter equations), and
A
the corresponding automaton as constructed in
Section

4
. Then
\mathrm{Sys}
is contractive iff
A
is loop-free.

Proof.

We sketch the proof for pushdown systems, since the other cases follow the same analysis. Observe that any configuration of the form
(q_{\mathrm{end}},n)
or
(q_{\mathrm{error}},n)
is already a reading configuration, so it cannot be the start of an infinite sequence of
\varepsilon
-moves. For the remaining configurations
(q_{X},\omega)
, it is clear by our construction that the sequence of
\varepsilon
-moves obtained by following the transition function is equivalent to a derivation attempt for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\omega}\rangle}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
following rules
C-z
and
C-s
, and that this sequence eventually reaches a reading configuration iff the derivation is successful. Thus we have an equivalence between systems for which all (variable, stack) pairs are contractive and automata for which all configurations eventually reach a reading configuration.
∎

Theorem 9
.

The following problems are decidable in polynomial time:

•

Given a system
\mathrm{Sys}
of recursive equations, is
\mathrm{Sys}
contractive?

•

Given a system
\mathrm{Sys}
of 1-counter equations, is
\mathrm{Sys}
contractive?

•

Given a system
\mathrm{Sys}
of pushdown equations, is
\mathrm{Sys}
contractive?

Proof.

In
Appendix

D
.
∎

Theorem 10
.

Problems
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{r}}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{1}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{p}}}}
are all decidable in
polynomial time.

Proof.

We only sketch the proof for the case of pushdown types, of which the other two can be seen as subcases. The algorithm described in the proof of
Theorem

9
not only determines whether a system of equations is contractive, but it can also be used to produce the set of “bad” type identifiers

\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{bad}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}\in\Delta\cup\{\varepsilon\}\;,\;\neg{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{contr}_{\mathsf{p}}}}\}.

Intuitively, a type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is well-formed (
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{p}}}}
) iff
the expansion of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
(which might be infinite) never visits a type identifier
in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{bad}}
. In particular, if the system is contractive, then

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\omega}\rangle}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{p}}}}
for any variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and stack contents
\omega
.
Otherwise, we can apply the construction in
Section

4
to
convert the system of pushdown equations into a deterministic pushdown automata;
we get that
\neg{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{p}}}}
iff there is a derivation

(q,\omega)\overset{w}{\rightarrow}(q_{X},\sigma)
for some

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{bad}}
. Now deciding whether such derivations exist can
be reduced to solving the reachability problem on deterministic pushdown
automata, which can be done in polynomial time (in fact, the problem even
remains polynomial-time solvable for nondeterministic pushdown automata). Here
is a short argument: one can change the automata in such a way that the only
accepting states are the states
(q_{X},\sigma)
corresponding to “bad”
configurations, reducing the problem to deciding if the pushdown automaton
accepts a non-empty language. Then, we can transform the automaton into a
context-free grammar
[
23
, Theorem 5.4]
. Finally, we can
use a polynomial-time algorithm
[
23
, Lemma 4.1]
to
decide if the language generated by a context-free grammar is non-empty.
∎

By making use of the known procedures for deciding equivalence of deterministic
automata, and since the construction in
Section

4
can be
implemented by a computable procedure, we can immediately derive decidability for
the corresponding problems for types.

Theorem 11
.

Problems
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{r}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{1}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{p}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
are all decidable.

Proof.

An algorithm for deciding type equivalence works as follows. First convert
each type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
into an equivalent automaton, following the steps in

Section

4
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
) is given by the initial
type identifier
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, then its corresponding automaton has

(q_{X},\omega)
as the initial configuration. By our construction, we get that

\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})
(resp.
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
) is the language accepted by the
corresponding automaton, and we can infer that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
iff (by

Proposition

2
)
\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}})=\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}})
iff the corresponding
automata are equivalent. We know that the equivalence of automata is decidable
for finite-state automata

[
22
,
36
]
,
1-counter automata

[
8
,
45
,
46
]
and
(deterministic) pushdown automata

[
38
,
39
]
.
Applying the corresponding algorithm gives us the desired answer.
∎

Building on type equivalence, we can establish similar results to decide whether
two types are the dual of each other. We start by building a dual to any type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
.

Lemma 8
.

For each class of types,
if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{type}
, then there exists

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\bot}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}}}
.

Proof.

We sketch the proof for pushdown types, as the other classes use essentially
the same idea. Consider a pushdown type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, say

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, with respect to a system
\mathrm{Sys}
of
pushdown equations, defined on a set of variables
\mathcal{X}
. We construct a dual
type
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{X}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
by extending the
system
\mathrm{Sys}
to a system
\mathrm{Sys}^{\prime}
, defined on the set of
variables
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{X}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{X}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{X}\}
. The
equations for the variables in
\mathcal{X}
are the same as in
\mathrm{Sys}
. The
equations for the duals of the variables in
\mathcal{X}
are given according to the
usual rules:

•

the dual of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
;

•

the dual of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T}}}.{U}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\sharp}T.\overline{U}}}
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{U}}}
is the dual of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
;

•

the dual of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{\star}{\{{\ell}\colon{\overline{T}_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T_{\ell}}}}
is the dual of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\ell}}}
;

•

additionally, the dual of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{X}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
.

A straightforward proof by coinduction then shows that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X}\langle{\omega}\rangle}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{p}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\overline{X}}\langle{\omega}\rangle}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}\in\mathcal{X}
and

\omega\in\Delta^{\ast}
, and thus
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{p}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}}}
.
∎

Notice that the proof above is constructive, i.e., given a system
\mathrm{Sys}
specifying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
, we can effectively produce a system
\mathrm{Sys}^{\prime}
specifying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}
.

Theorem 12
.

Problems
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{r}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{1}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{p}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
are all decidable.

Proof.

Given the types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, construct
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}
according to the proof of
Lemma

8
. Then decide whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
are equivalent by applying the procedure in the proof of
Theorem

11
.
∎

In
Section

4
we show how to convert a system of equations into an automaton, which enables us to prove that certain problems on types are decidable by observing that their counterparts for automata are decidable. Similarly, our reverse construction from automata to systems of equations in
Section

5
allows to prove that certain problems on types are undecidable since their counterparts for automata are undecidable.

Theorem 13

(Undecidability results)
.

Problems
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{2}}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
are all undecidable.

Proof.

For
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{2}}}}
we start from the following undecidable
problem (essentially, the halting problem): given a description of a one-tape
Turing machine
M
, and starting from an empty tape, determine whether the
machine reaches a given state
q
. We reduce from this problem into the
problem of deciding whether a given type identifier is contractive. Apply the
constructions in Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
, Chapter 7]
(already mentioned in
our
Theorem

3
) to convert
M
into a 2-counter automaton

A
. The construction yields an initial configuration
c_{0}
and a final state

q^{\prime}
in
A
such that
q^{\prime}
is reachable from
c_{0}
by
\varepsilon
-moves iff
the machine
M
reaches
q
. Now apply the construction at the end of

Section

5
to construct the associated system of 2-counter
equations, but making the replacement
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X_{q^{\prime}}}\langle{N,M}\rangle}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}
for all
equations corresponding to state
q^{\prime}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{n_{0},m_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
be the type
identifier corresponding to configuration
c_{0}
. We get that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{2}}}}

iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
iff
q^{\prime}
is reachable from
c
by
\varepsilon
-moves iff
the machine
M
reaches
q
, concluding the reduction.

For
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
we can simply observe that type equivalence builds on contractivity (cf. the 2-counter
type formation rule correspondent to rule
T-Id
,
Fig.

2
).
Thus, to decide type equivalence one must decide contractiveness, which we have just shown to be undecidable. Alternatively, we can show that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
is undecidable without resorting to the undecidability of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
(which suggests that the problem is ‘harder’ than contractiveness). To do that, we start from the following undecidable
problem: given two decidable languages
E
,
F
, determine whether
E=F
(this
is undecidable even for context-free languages, as shown by
Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
]
). Without loss fix a computable encoding between
the words in the language and natural numbers, so that we can assume that

E,F\subseteq\mathbb{N}
. Next, consider the types
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{E}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{F}}}
given
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{E}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp_{0}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}.\sharp_{1}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}.\sharp_{2}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}\ldots}}
, where
\sharp_{n}
is
either
?
if
n\in E
or
!
if
n\not\in E
, and similarly for

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{F}}}
. Since
E,F
are decidable, so are

\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{E}}}),\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{F}}})
; thus, by

Theorem

5
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{E}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{2}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{F}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{2}}}}
.
Observing that
E=F
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{E}}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{F}}}}
concludes the reduction.

For
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
we reduce from type equivalence, noting
that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\mathrm{{\bot}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}}\;{\mathrm{{\simeq}_{\mathsf{2}}}}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\overline{T}}}
is
the type constructed from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
according to the proof of

Lemma

8
.
∎

9
Related work

The first papers on session types by Honda
[
20
]
and Takeuchi et al.
[
41
]
feature finite types only. Recursive types
were introduced later
[
21
]
using
\mu
-notation. Gay and Hole

[
16
]
introduce algorithms for deciding duality and
subtyping of finite-state session types, based on bisimulation. Much of the
literature on session types, surveyed by Hüttel et al.
[
24
]
, uses the same approach. The
natural decision algorithms for duality and subtyping presented by Gay and
Hole were shown to be exponential in the
size of the types by Lange and Yoshida
[
30
]
, due to reliance on syntactic unfolding. Our polytime
complexity for recursive type equivalence follows from the equivalence algorithm for finite-state automata by Hopcroft and Karp
[
22
]
, and thus has quadratic complexity in the description size, which is an improvement on that of Gay and Hole. Lange and Yoshida use an
automata-based algorithm to also achieve quadratic complexity for checking
subtyping.

We use a coinductive formulation of infinite session types. This approach has
some connections with the work of Keizer et al.
[
26
]
who
present session types as states of coalgebras. Their types are restricted to
finite-state recursive types, but they do address subtyping and non-linear
types, two notions that we do not take into consideration. Our coinductive
presentation avoids explicitly building coalgebras, and follows
Gay et al.
[
17
]
, solving problems with duality in the
presence of recursive
types
[
5
,
17
,
31
]
.

This paper does not address the problem of deciding subtyping, but the panorama
is not promising. Subtyping is known to be decidable for recursive types

\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{r}}

[
16
]
and undecidable for context-free types
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{c}}

[
34
]
or nested types with arity at
most one
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{n}}^{1}

[
11
]
, hence for
pushdown types with one type constructor
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}
(
Theorem

2
).
The undecidability proof of the subtyping problem for context-free session
types reduces from the inclusion problem for simple deterministic languages, which was shown to be undecidable by Friedman
[
14
]
.
That for nested session types reduces from the inclusion problem for Basic Process
Algebra
[
4
]
, which was shown to be undecidable by
Groote and Hüttel
[
19
]
.
Given that 1-counter types
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{1}}
and pushdown types with one type
constructor
\mathbb{T}_{\mathsf{p}}^{1}
are incomparable (
Theorem

2
), the problem
of subtyping for 1-counter types remains open.

Dependent session types have been studied in several forms, for binary session types
[
43
,
44
]
, for multi-party session types
[
13
,
32
,
48
]
and for polymorphic, nested session types
[
10
]
.
Although our parameterised type definitions have some similarities with definitions in some dependently typed systems, we do not support the connection between values in messages and parameters in types, and we have not yet studied how the types that can be expressed in dependent systems fit into our hierarchy.

Connections between multiparty session types and communicating finite-state
automata have been explored by Deniélou and Yoshida
[
12
]
but the
investigation has not been extended to other classes of automata.

Solomon
[
40
]
studies the connection between inductive type
equality for nested types and language equality for DPDAs and shows that the
equivalence problem for nested types is as hard as the equivalence problem for
DPDAs, an open problem at the time. We follow a similar approach but take type
equivalence coinductively, as a bisimulation, rather than as a problem of
language equivalence.

Many of the main results in this paper borrow from the theory of automata,
developed in the mid-20th century. Here our standard reference is the book by
Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
]
, where the notions of finite-state automata,
pushdown automata, and counter automata can be found. 1-counter automata were
studied in detail in Valiant’s PhD thesis

[
45
]
. To prove the equivalence between types and
automata, we need to convert automata into equivalent ones satisfying certain
properties; similar techniques have appeared in Kao et al.
[
25
]
and Valiant and Paterson
[
46
]
. Our
proofs of decidability of type equivalence make use of the corresponding results
for automata

[
8
,
22
,
36
,
38
,
39
,
46
]
;
we specifically mention Sénizergues’ impressive result on the decidability of
equivalence for deterministic pushdown automata

[
39
]
, a work which granted
him the Gödel Prize in 2002. Finally, the strict hierarchy results use textbook
pumping lemmas for regular languages (due to Rabin and Scott
[
36
]
) and
context-free languages (due to
Bar-Hillel et al.
[
3
]
and
Kreowski
[
29
]
), as well as a somewhat less known result for
1-counter automata (due to Boasson
[
7
]
).

10
Conclusion

We introduce different classes of session types, some new, others from the
literature, under a uniform framework and place them in an hierarchy. We further
study different type-related problems—formation, equivalence and duality—and
show that these relations are all decidable up to and including pushdown types.

Much remains to be done. From the point of view of programming languages, one
should investigate whether decidability results translate into algorithms that
may be incorporated in compilers.
Even if subtyping is known to be undecidable for most systems “above” that of
recursive types, the problem remains open for 1-counter types, an interesting
avenue for further investigation.
Our study of classes of infinite types may have applications beyond session
types. One promising direction is that of non regular datatypes for functional
programming (or polymorphic recursion schemes

[
33
]
), such as nested datatypes

[
6
]
.

We have not
addressed the decidability of the type checking problem. Type checking is known to be decidable for finite types, recursive, context-free
and nested session types.
Given that type checking for nested session types is incorporated in the RAST language

[
10
]
, a natural first step would be to investigate how to translate 1-counter and pushdown processes into that language.

Appendix

Appendix A
Proof of
Theorem

1
(Embedding from context-free to infinite session types)

To prove the embedding theorem, we use the classical coinduction principle for set-based
coalgebras
[
28
]
.

Theorem
1

(Embedding)
.

1.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
, then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{\infty}}}}
.

2.

If
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
, then there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
.

3.

Suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. Then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
iff
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
.

Proof.

For
Item

1
, we proceed coinductively on the structure of the proof of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. We illustrate some relevant cases:

•

Suppose a proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
ends with rule
Emb-Skip
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
. We know that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
by
T-Skip
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{\infty}}}}
by
T-End
.

•

Suppose a proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
ends with rule
Emb-Id
. Then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
