with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. By coinduction,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{\infty}}}}
. By rule
T-Id
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
as well.

•

Suppose a proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
ends with rule
Emb-SemiChoice
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}};{T^{\prime}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
. We also have that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}
for all
\ell\in L
. By coinduction,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{\infty}}}}
for all
\ell\in L
. By rule
T-Choice
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{\infty}}}}
as well.
Moreover, the proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
must end with rule
T-Semi
, so that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\ell}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
for all
\ell\in L
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
. Therefore, by rules
T-Choice
and
T-Semi
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}};{T^{\prime}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
as well.

•

Suppose a proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
ends with rule
Emb-SemiSemi
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}})};{T_{3}}}}
and we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
.
By coinduction,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{\infty}}}}
.
The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
must end with rule
T-Semi
, so that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
.
Similarly, we have that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{3}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
. By rule
T-Semi
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}})};{T_{3}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
as well.

For
Item

2
, we proceed coinductively on the structure of the proof of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
. We illustrate some relevant cases.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T^{\prime}}}}
. Then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and by coinduction, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U^{\prime}}}}
. Then, by rule
Emb-Msg
, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{T^{\prime}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sharp\,{U^{\prime}}}}.{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}}}}}}}
.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
, with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
. By coinduction, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. By rule
Emb-Id
, we then have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}}
. Here we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
, and again we proceed by coinduction on that proof (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is either
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
, message passing, choice, a variable, or sequential composition). Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
. For each
\ell\in L
, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{\ell}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and also (due to rule
Emb-Skip
)
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T_{2}}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
. By coinduction, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T_{2}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}
for each
\ell\in L
. We then have by rule
Emb-SemiChoice
that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}};{T_{2}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}
.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}})};{T_{3}}}}
. We have that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{1}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{2}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{3}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
, so that also
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\>\mathrm{\mathrm{{type}_{\mathsf{c}}}}
. Taking
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
, we have by rule
Emb-SemiSemi
that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}})};{T_{3}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
.

For
Item

3
, we proceed coinductively on the structure of the proofs for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. Note that there are nine possible rules for each proof, so there are eighty-one cases in total. We illustrate some relevant cases.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}};{T^{\prime}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
. The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
must have ended with rule
Emb-SemiSkip
, so that we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
must have ended with rule
Emb-Skip
, so that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
. In the forward direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. Given the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
, its proof must have ended with rule
E-Neut1L
, and so
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. Then, by coinduction, we get
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. In the converse direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. By coinduction, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. Then, by rule
E-Neut1L
we get
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}};{T^{\prime}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{V_{2}}}})};{V_{3}}}}
. The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
must have ended with rule
Emb-SemiChoice
, so that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}
for each
\ell\in L
. By rule
Emb-Choice
, this means that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{\ell}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}
. The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
must have ended with rule
Emb-SemiSemi
, so we must have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
.

In the forward direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. Given the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
, we must have ended that proof with either rule
E-Semi
, or rules
E-DistL
and
E-AssocR
. In the case that rule
E-Semi
was used, we would get
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{V_{2}}}}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{3}}}}
. By a coinductive argument, we can derive that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{\ell}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}
. On the other hand, if we used rules
E-DistL
and
E-AssocR
, we would also arrive at
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{\ell}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}
. In either case, we would conclude by coinduction that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{U_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
, that is,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
.

In the converse direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. Given the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}
, we must have ended that proof with rule
E-Choice
, which means that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{W_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U_{\ell}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W_{\ell}}}}
for each
\ell\in L
. Thus
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{W_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}
. A coinductive argument shows that in this situation, there exist
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{\ell}}}
for
\ell\in L
with
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{V_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{\ell}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W_{\ell}}}}
. By coinduction, we then conclude that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{\ell}}}}
for all
\ell\in L
. Then, by rule
E-Choice
we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{\ell}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{V_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}}}}
. We then have the chain of equivalences

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{T_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}};{T^{\prime}}}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}\{{\ell}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{\ell}};{T^{\prime}}}}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\star}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\{{\ell}\colon{V_{\ell}}\}_{{\ell}\in{L}}}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{V_{2}}}})};{V_{3}}}},

so that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
as desired.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}};{T^{\prime\prime}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}
. The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
must have ended with rule
Emb-SemiId
, so we must have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T^{\prime}};{T^{\prime\prime}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}
. The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
must have ended with rule
Emb-Id
, so we must have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}}\;{\doteq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}\>\mathrm{contr}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}
.

In the forward direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. By examining the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
, we have three possibilities for the last rule used. The first possibility is that rule
E-Neut2L
was used (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime\prime}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
). Then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. We can prove (by coinduction) that if
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T^{\prime}};{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T^{\prime}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. Therefore by coinduction, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. The second possibility is that rule
E-IdSemiL
was used. In this case we would have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T^{\prime}};{T^{\prime\prime}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. Again by coinduction, we conclude
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. The third possibility is that rule
E-ConsR
was used. In this case we would have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}
. Again by coinduction, we conclude that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
.

In the converse direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. By coinduction, this implies that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T^{\prime}};{T^{\prime\prime}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V^{\prime}}}}
. Applying rules
E-IdSemiL
and
E-ConsR
would then enable us to conclude that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
as desired.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}})};{T_{3}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{V_{2}}}})};{V_{3}}}}
. The proof for
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
must have ended with rule
Emb-SemiSemi
, so we must have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. Similarly, we must have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
.

In the forward direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. By examining the structure of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}
, we have five possibilities for the last rule used. The first possibility is that rule
E-Neut2L
was used (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{3}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
). Then
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
. We can prove (by coinduction) that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}}}}}\;{\hookrightarrow}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}
. Therefore by coinduction, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. The second possibility is that rule
E-Neut2R
was used (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{3}}}
is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{skip}}}
). The reasoning is analogous. The third possibility is that rule
E-Semi
was used. In this case we would have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{V_{2}}}}}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T_{3}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V_{3}}}}
. A coinductive argument shows that these two equivalences imply
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}
. Therefore by coincudtion, we have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. The fourth possibility is that rule
E-AssocL
was used. In this case we get
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{V_{2}}}})};{V_{3}}}}}}}
. Again by coinduction, we conclude
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. The fifth possibility is that rule
E-AssocR
was used. The reasoning is analogous.

In the converse direction, suppose
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{U}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{W}}}
. By coinduction, this implies
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}}}}
. We have the chain of equivalences

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{T_{2}}}})};{T_{3}}}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{T_{2}};{T_{3}}}})}}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{2}};{V_{3}}}})}}}\simeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{V_{1}};{V_{2}}}})};{V_{3}}}},

so that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}}\;{\simeq}\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{V}}}
as desired.

∎

Appendix B
Proof of
Theorem

3
(Normal form automata)

Theorem
3

(Normal form automata)
.

•

Any finite-state automaton can be converted into an equivalent normal form automaton.

•

Any 1-counter automaton can be converted into an equivalent normal form automaton.

•

Any pushdown automaton can be converted into an equivalent normal form automaton.

•

Any decidable language is accepted by a 2-counter normal form automaton.

Proof.

For finite-state automata, there are well-known techniques to convert any automaton into an
automaton without
\varepsilon
-moves
[
23
, Section
2.4]
, which is trivially in normal form. For pushdown
automata, this result is a consequence of a result in Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
, Section 10.3
and Exercise 10.7]
. The remaining two cases have not, to
the best of our knowledge, been considered in the literature. Note in
particular that the result for 1-counter automaton does not immediately follow
from the result for pushdown automaton, since the construction of an
equivalent pushdown automaton in normal form presented by Hopcroft and Ullman extends the stack alphabet with new symbols.

We begin with the case of decidable languages. Let
L
be a decidable language. In other words, there is a Turing machine
M
with two distinguished final states (
q_{\mathrm{accept}}
and
q_{\mathrm{reject}}
) such that for any word
w
written in the input tape of
M
,

if
w\in L
, then
M
terminates in state
q_{\mathrm{accept}}
; and if
w\not\in L
, then
M
terminates in state
q_{\mathrm{reject}}
.

By using standard techniques in the theory of Turing machines, we can assume that:

•

the machine has a read-only input tape, whose head can only move in one direction;

•

the machine has a single working tape.

From this we can construct a new machine
M^{\prime}
that ‘knows’ (i.e., by a suitable
encoding on its finite control) whether a word
w
is in
L
immediately after
reading the last symbol of
w
. The idea is that machine
M^{\prime}
stores in the
working tape the contents of the input word
w^{\prime}
read thus far; before reading
the next input symbol,
M^{\prime}
simulates
M
for all possible immediate
continuations of
w^{\prime}
, storing in the finite control which simulations resulted
in acceptance. Given this machine
M^{\prime}
, we use the construction in Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
, Lemma
7.3]
to obtain an equivalent two-stack machine. Because

M^{\prime}
knows whether a word
w
is in
L
immediately after reading the last
symbol of
w
, the resulting two-stack machine can be ensured to be in normal
form, i.e., it can immediately accept
w
after reading its last symbol. Finally,
we apply the constructions described in Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
, Lemma 7.4 and Theorem
7.9]
to convert this two-stack automaton into an equivalent
four-counter automaton and subsequently a two-counter automaton. All these
constructions essentially simulate a single stack or counter operation by a
sequence of counter operations, and thus they do not interfere with the
semantics of reading moves. In other words, the resulting automata are also
guaranteed to be in normal form.

The only case left is that of one-counter automata, and here the proof is more extensive. Let
A
be a 1-counter automaton. Our proof will become simpler if we assume that, for every state
q
,
(q,\mathsf{z})
is a reading mode iff
(q,\mathsf{s})
is a reading mode.
A
can be converted in this form by creating additional states
q_{\varepsilon}
for each state
q
that can be a reading mode or an
\varepsilon
-mode depending on the value of the counter. Hence, from now on we assume this property of
A
; in particular, we can talk about reading states and
\varepsilon
-states instead of reading modes and
\varepsilon
-modes.

Now observe that each choice of reading state
q
, counter value
n
, and input symbol
a
, defines a unique path from the configuration
(q,n)
that reads
a
and either takes
\varepsilon
-moves forever or ends in some reading configuration
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. In other words, we can define a function

F:Q\times\mathbb{N}\times\Sigma\rightarrow\{\mathrm{accept},\mathrm{reject}\}\times(\{\uparrow\}\cup Q\times\mathbb{N})

such that
F(q,n,a)
precisely captures the unique behaviour of the automaton from configuration
(q,n)
after reading
a
. In particular, the first component of
F(q,n,a)
is ‘accept’ if the aforementioned path of
\varepsilon
-moves visits some accepting state, and ‘reject’ otherwise. The second component of
F(q,n,a)
is
\uparrow
if the aforementioned path is infinite, or
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
if it ends in that reading configuration. Notice also that
F
essentially tells us all we need to know about the automaton, since it specifies how we move from a reading state into the next reading state.

For
(q,n)\in Q\times\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let us use the notation
(q,n)+k
to denote the configuration
(q,n+k)
. Similarly, when
z
is either ‘accept’ or ‘reject’, let us use the notation
(z,(q,n))+k
to denote
(z,(q,n+k))
. The first key idea of the proof is the following characterisation of the sections of
F
.

Claim 1
.

For each reading state
q
and input symbol
a
, the function
F(q,\cdot,a)
(with domain
\mathbb{N}
) must be one of the following two types:

•

there exist integers
K,K^{\prime}
such that
F(q,n,a)=F(q,n^{\prime},a)
for all
n,n^{\prime}\geq K
with
n=n^{\prime}\mod K^{\prime}
;

•

there exists an integer
K
such that
F(q,n,a)=F(q,n^{\prime},a)+(n-n^{\prime})
for all
n\geq n^{\prime}\geq K
.

To prove this claim, consider the path that starts from state
q
, reading
a
, and continues with
\varepsilon
-moves while taking the branches associated with non-zero counter value.

•

Suppose this path reaches a reading state
q^{\prime}
. Then there is a minimal value
K
such that any configuration
(q,n)
with
n\geq K
would follow this path of moves. Hence, the decision between acceptance and rejection would be the same for all such
n
. Moreover, the difference in the counter values at
(q,n)
and at the end of the path is the same for every such
n\geq K
. Hence, we fit into the second case of the claim.

•

Suppose this path revisits an
\varepsilon
-state
q^{\prime}
. Consider the first two occurrences of this state, and the change of the counter value between these two occurrences. If the change is non-negative, i.e., the counter value on the second occurrence is not smaller, then all configurations
(q,n)
with
n
large enough will lead to infinite looping paths that visit the same set of states. Hence, the decision between acceptance and rejection would be the same, and we fit into the first case of the claim with
K^{\prime}=1
(since
n=n^{\prime}\mod 1
is trivially satisfied).

•

Suppose again that this path revisits an
\varepsilon
-state
q^{\prime}
, but now the change of the counter value between these two occurences is negative. Let
K^{\prime}
be the decrease associated with this loop of
\varepsilon
-moves, and let
K
be a value such that any configuration
(q,n)
with
n\geq K
would follow this path of moves up to the second occurrence of
q^{\prime}
. Then, for any
\ell\geq 0
, the path of moves starting with a configuration of the form
(q,n+\ell K^{\prime})
would be identical to the path associated to
(q,n)
, but with an additional intermediate sequence of
\ell
loops, each of which decrements the counter value by
K^{\prime}
. Hence, we fit into the first case of the claim.

Now that we have our claim proven, we extend the above characterisation in a way that the constants
K,K^{\prime}
do not depend on the reading state
q
or the input symbol
a
. We can simply take
K
to be the maximum of the corresponding values of
K
obtained by the claim, and
K^{\prime}
to be the lowest common multiple of the corresponding values. Therefore, we have found constants
K,K^{\prime}
(that are fixed for a given automaton), such that for every reading state
q
and input symbol
a
, the function
F(q,\cdot,a)
fits into one of the above types.

Now that we have a global value of
K^{\prime}
, we can convert our automaton into an equivalent one, but for which the value of
K^{\prime}
may be assumed to be
1
(i.e., the first case of the claim reduces to the statement that
F(q,n,a)=F(q,n^{\prime},a)
for all
n,n^{\prime}\geq K
). To achieve this, we essentially create
K^{\prime}
copies of each state of the original automaton; each state is now of the form
(q,k)
, where
q
encodes the original state and
k
encodes the current equivalence class modulus
K^{\prime}
of the counter value. Any transition that increments or decrements the counter now also moves to the corresponding equivalence class, and for each combination of reading state
q
, input symbol
a
, and equivalence class
k\mod K^{\prime}
, such that
F(q,\cdot,a)
is of the first type (according to the original automaton), we change the transition function so that (for the new automaton)
F((q,k),n,a)=F((q,k),n^{\prime},a)
for every
n,n^{\prime}\geq K
.

In summary, we can assume at this stage that our 1-counter automaton
A
has the following property: there exists a constant
K
such that, for every reading state
q
and input symbol
a
, either:
F(q,n,a)=F(q,K,a)
for every
n\geq K
; or
F(q,n,a)=F(q,K,a)+(n-K)
for every
n\geq K
. From this assumption, we can now construct our automaton
A^{\prime}
in normal form as follows. Essentially, the new automaton
A^{\prime}
has a state
q
for each of the reading states
q
in
A
. We will create additional states and transitions such that
A^{\prime}
postpones the reading of the next symbol, while simulating the computation of
\varepsilon
-moves of
A
.

When reaching a state
q
that was originally a reading state in
A
, the automaton
A^{\prime}
now proceeds by checking whether the counter value
n
is one of
0,1,\ldots,K-1
, or greater than
K
(this can be done with a sequence of
K
states linked by
\varepsilon
-moves). For each case, we can immediately decide whether a given symbol
a
should lead to an accepting or rejecting state, by looking at the first component of
F(q,\min(n,K),a)
. Moreover, the second component of
F(q,\min(n,K),a)
is either
\uparrow
or another configuration
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. We can handle the case
\uparrow
by including a transition to a fresh non-accepting state, for which all transitions are reading self-loop moves. This also makes the automaton guaranteed to read. If, on the other hand, the second component of
F(q,\min(n,K),a)
is a configuration
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
, then we can handle this case by adding a sequence of states and
\varepsilon
-transitions that update the counter value accordingly. For each of the finitely many cases where
n<K
, we simply create a sequence of
\varepsilon
-transitions that increment the counter value. For the case that
n\geq K
and
F(q,\cdot,a)
is of the first type, we introduce an
\varepsilon
-self-loop that resets the counter to zero, and then a sequence of
\varepsilon
-transitions that end up at
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. For the case that
n\geq K
and
F(q,\cdot,a)
is of the second type, we create a sequence of
\varepsilon
-transitions that increment the counter by
n^{\prime}
and then move to state
q^{\prime}
. This ensures that the automaton transitions to the configuration
(q^{\prime},n^{\prime}+(n-K))
as desired. Thus, the resulting automaton
A^{\prime}
is equivalent to
A
and is in normal form.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of
Theorem

8
(Equivalence of pushdown and nested session types)

Theorem
8
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
be a nested session type using type constructors of arity at most
n
. Then, there is a pushdown session type representation for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
using only
n
type variables.

Proof.

Given
Lemma

6
, we can assume
n\geq 2
. The proof is significantly more elaborate than the proof of
Lemma

6
. The reason is that general nesting of
n
-ary operators gives rise to an evaluation tree, which is more complex than a sequential composition of operators. However, due to the restricted way in which nesting can occur, it is still possible to represent these evaluation trees using a single stack.

Formally, consider a representation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
using the type constructors
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}}
, …,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(m)}}}
, where each type constructor has arity at most
n
. Again, without loss of generality we can assume that the arguments in the equation defining a
k
-ary type constructor are
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{k}}}
in that order. These equations may have arbitrarily nested expressions on their right-hand side, for example we could have an expression like
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. Let
d
denote the highest depth of any nested expression appearing in any equation of the representation (the previous example has a depth of
2
).
Let
\mathcal{E}_{d}
denote all possible nested expressions of depth at most
d
. This can also be seen as the space of rooted trees of depth at most
d
where internal nodes (having
k
children, for
0<k\leq n
) are labelled by a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(i)}}}
(of arity
k
), and leaves are labelled either by a variable
\alpha_{j}
or a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(j)}}}
of arity
0
. Since the depth
d
, the maximum arity
n
and the number of type constructors
m
are all finite, the number of such expressions is finite (albeit exponentially large; later we will argue that at most polynomially many trees need to be considered).

Our stack alphabet is then defined to be
\Delta=\mathcal{E}_{d}\cup\mathcal{E}_{d}^{n}
, that is, the union of
\mathcal{E}_{d}
with the space of
n
-tuples of expressions in
\mathcal{E}_{d}
, which is again a finite set. As it shall be seen in the proof, the intuition is that a stack symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{d}
captures the current, top-level expression, and it can appear only at the top of the stack; whereas a stack symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{d}^{n}
captures the possible continuations at the leaves of the tree, and it usually appears below the top level of the stack. Moreover, we will use (pushdown) type constructors
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{n}}}
in our representation, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}
intuitively means that our evaluation continues with the
i
-th element of the tuple
\sigma
. Additionally,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}
has the double duty of unfolding the top-level expression.

Formally, we need to define the right-hand side of equation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
for the case that
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{d}
, as well as the right-hand side of equation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{d}^{n}
. The remaining cases (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
for
j\neq 1
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{d}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
) are not of concern, as they will not be reached by our construction; for completeness, we could define the right-hand sides of those cases to be
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}}
.

Let us start with the case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
with
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{d}
.
\sigma
is either a variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{j}}}
or a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(j)}}}
applied with zero or more subexpressions. If
\sigma
is a variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{j}}}
, we pop our stack and continue with type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}
. In other words, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{j}S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

Suppose now that
\sigma
is a
k
-ary type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(j)}}}
applied to subexpressions
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}
. The nested session type representation includes an equation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(j)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. We will define

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tilde{T}}},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tilde{T}}}
is obtained from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
by performing an appropriate replacement on all nested expressions appearing in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
. Let
\bar{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k},\varepsilon,\ldots,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{E}_{d}^{n}
denote the
n
-tuple whose first
k
components are the subexpressions
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}
, and the remaining
(n-k)
components are empty subexpressions. Then, every (maximal) nested expression
\sigma^{\prime}
appearing in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}
is replaced by the type variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma^{\prime}\bar{\sigma}S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. In this way,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\tilde{T}}}
is a valid right-hand side for pushdown session types representations.

The case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
where
j=1,\ldots,n
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{E}_{d}^{n}
is straightforward: if
\sigma_{j}
is the
j
-th compontent of the tuple
\sigma
, then we define

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{j}S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

Finally, suppose the initial type is given by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}
. Then, in our pushdown representation,
8
8

8

Here we are assuming that the depth of
\sigma
is also at most
d
, the maximum depth of any expression appearing in the equational specification. This is without loss, as otherwise we could just apply the same construction but for a depth
d^{\prime}
which is the maximum between
d
and the depth of
\sigma
.
the corresponding initial type is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. To finish the proof, we need to show that the representation described above gives rise to the same type
T
. This can be proven coinductively by observing that both unfoldings follow the same type constructs, and that the stack encoding explained above is enough to express the expressions appearing while unfolding the nested session type representation.
∎

Although the proof above increases the number of equations by an superexponential multiplicative factor (there are
O((m+n)^{n^{d}})
expressions in
\mathcal{E}_{d}
and
O((m+n)^{n^{d+1}})
stack symbols), we remark here that the construction can be altered to have only a polynomial overhead. The reasoning is that there are polynomially many expressions appearing in the original nested session type representation (in fact, linearly in the encoding size of the representation); each of the expressions has polynomially many subexpressions (in fact, linearly in its encoding size). We only need to consider the case that the stack symbol is one of these subexpressions, or the tuple corresponding to its direct children. Thus, at most linearly many equations need to be considered for each equation in the original nested session type representation.

We illustrate the above proof with an example of this simulation. Since the simulation creates a large number of equations, we will only show an initial fragment of the unfolding steps, and a subset of relevant equations as we go along. In our example there are four type constructors
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}}
(arity 3),
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}
(arity 2) and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}}}
(arity 0). Therefore, the maximum arity is
n=3
. Suppose the nested session type equations are

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\{\mathsf{stop}:\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}},\mathsf{go}:{X^{(2)}}\langle{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle}\rangle\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\{\mathsf{left}:\alpha_{1},\mathsf{right}:\alpha_{2}\}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

One can see that the maximum depth in subexpressions is
d=2
. By looking at the initial type, our pushdown representation begins with
9
9

9

Pay close attention to the notation: here
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}
is the type constructor, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(1)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the stack symbol.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{T}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(1)}}\langle{X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)}}\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

Next, applying the technique in the proof of
Theorem

8
, we see that the pushdown representation would have an equation

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(1)}}\langle{X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)}}\rangle\cdot S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\{\mathsf{stop}:\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{end}}},\mathsf{go}:{X_{1}}\langle{\sigma^{\prime}\bar{\sigma}S}\rangle\}}}

\displaystyle\text{with }\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\text{and }\bar{\sigma}

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}}})

Suppose we unfold the type, and take the branch
\mathsf{go}
, that is, we take the transition labeled
\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\mathsf{go}
. Then, we arrive at type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(2)}}\langle{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle}\rangle\cdot(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

From here, we continue by looking at one of the equations corresponding to the type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}
. Namely, we observe that the pushdown representation must include the equation

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(2)}}\langle{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(4)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle}\rangle\cdot S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\{\mathsf{left}:{X_{1}}\langle{\alpha_{1}\bar{\sigma}S}\rangle,\mathsf{right}:{X_{1}}\langle{\alpha_{2}\bar{\sigma}S}\rangle\}}}

\displaystyle\text{with }\bar{\sigma}

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}},\varepsilon)

Suppose we unfold the type, taking the transition
\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\mathsf{left}
. We would arrive at the type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1}\cdot({X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle,\varepsilon)\cdot(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

From here the next two steps are straightforward: first, we take the equation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{1}S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, arriving at the type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{({X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle,\varepsilon)\cdot(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

Afterwards, we take the equation

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{({X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle,{X^{(2)}}\langle{X^{(3)},\alpha_{1}}\rangle,\varepsilon)\cdot S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle\cdot S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

to arrive at the type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle\cdot(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

At this point, we again look at one of the equations corresponding to the type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(2)}}}
, namely

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(2)}}\langle{\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2}}\rangle\cdot S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

\displaystyle\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\{\mathsf{left}:{X_{1}}\langle{\alpha_{1}\bar{\sigma}S}\rangle,\mathsf{right}:{X_{1}}\langle{\alpha_{2}\bar{\sigma}S}\rangle\}}}

\displaystyle\text{with }\bar{\sigma}

\displaystyle=({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{3}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{2}}},\varepsilon)

Suppose this time we take the transition
\hskip-0.43057pt\oplus\hskip-0.43057pt\mathsf{right}
, arriving at the type

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{2}\cdot(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2},\varepsilon)\cdot(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

The next few steps are again straightforward: we move to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{(\alpha_{3},\alpha_{2},\varepsilon)\cdot(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

and then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha_{2}\cdot(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

and then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{(X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

and finally

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

Let us take one more transition to conclude the example. Here we would look at an equation corresponding to the constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}}}
, namely

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{(3)}\cdot S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(1)}}\langle{X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)}}\rangle S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}

which would take us back to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{{X^{(1)}}\langle{X^{(3)},X^{(3)},X^{(3)}}\rangle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}.

One can observe that the transitions taken in the pushdown session type representation match exactly the transitions valid for the nested session type representation, so that they are equivalent.

Appendix D
Proof of
Theorem

9

(Decidability of contractivity)

Theorem
9
.

The following problems are decidable in polynomial time:

•

Given a system
\mathrm{Sys}
of recursive equations, is
\mathrm{Sys}
contractive?

•

Given a system
\mathrm{Sys}
of 1-counter equations, is
\mathrm{Sys}
contractive?

•

Given a system
\mathrm{Sys}
of pushdown equations, is
\mathrm{Sys}
contractive?

Proof.

It should be clear that the construction in
Section

4
can be implemented in polynomial time, for each of the three equation schemes.
Lemma

7
establishes that
\mathrm{Sys}
is contractive iff the corresponding automaton is loop-free. The decision problem for loop-freeness is well-known to be decidable. For example, Hopcroft and Ullman
[
23
, Section 2.4]
describe a procedure to convert a finite-state automaton with
\varepsilon
-moves into an equivalent automaton without
\varepsilon
-moves. Similarly, Valiant
[
45
, Lemma 2.4]
shows how to convert a (deterministic) pushdown automaton into an equivalent loop-free automaton (which therefore works also for 1-counter automata). In both cases, the procedures detect infinite sequences of
\varepsilon
-moves if they exist, and thus, they can be used to decide whether a given automaton is loop-free. It can also be established from those proofs that the running time is polynomial in the description of the automaton.

However, it will be useful for
Theorem

10
to have algorithms with certain desirable properties; hence, we devote the rest of the proof to presenting an algorithm that decides if a system of pushdown equations is contractive in time
\mathcal{O}(|Q||\Delta|)
; since recursive equations and 1-counter equations can be seen as special cases, this also implies a polynomial time algorithm for those systems.

Our algorithm works as follows. Let us say that an equation is
trivial
if its right-hand side is a type constructor, e.g.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\alpha S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}\doteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{Y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{S}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. Suppose the system is not contractive. Then there exists an infinite sequence
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, …of type identifiers obtained by following trivial equations. By looking at the stack lengths along this sequence, one of two properties must hold: either some stack length is repeated infinitely often, or each stack length value occurs only finitely many times in the sequence.

Suppose some value of the stack length is repeated infinitely often. Let
n
be the minimum such value (for simplicity, let us assume
n>0
; the argumentation for
n=0
is essentially the same). There is some order
N
such that
|\omega_{i}|\geq n
for every
i\geq N
. In particular, this implies that all such
\omega_{i}
for
i\geq N
coincide on the bottom
n-1
stack symbols. Let
\omega
be the bottom substack of size
n-1
of these
\omega_{i}
. Now, consider all the type identifiers
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
for which
|\omega_{i}|=n
and
i\geq n
, which by assumption occur for infinitely many
i
. Notice that all such
\omega_{i}
coincide except possibly on their topmost symbols. Since there are finitely many possible type variables in the system, and finitely many stack symbols, an immediate application of the pigeonhole principle yields that there must be a type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and a stack symbol
\sigma
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and
\omega_{i}=\sigma\omega
for at least two different
i
(in fact, for infinitely many
i
). This means that, if we were to start a sequence of type identifiers with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, we would eventually return to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
(as we would never have to observe the contents of the stack below this position), and thus be stuck in an infinite loop. Similarly, if
n=0
was the minimum such value, then some type identifier
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is repeated at least twice (in fact, infinitely often), and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
would accordingly be the start of an infinite sequence.

Now suppose each stack length occurs only finitely many times in the sequence. For each
n
, let
i_{n}
denote the last time that the stack length is
n
. Notice that
i_{n}
is defined for all
n
sufficiently large. Via the same pigeonhole principle as before, there must exist some type constructor
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and stack symbol
\sigma
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i_{n}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and
\sigma
is the top symbol of
\omega_{i_{n}}
for at least two different
n,n^{\prime}
(in fact, for infinitely many
n
). Assuming without loss that
n<n^{\prime}
, and since
i_{n}
was the last time that the stack length was
n
, there must be stack words
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
of size
n-1
,
n^{\prime}-n
respectively such that
\omega_{i_{n}}=\sigma w
and
\omega_{i_{n^{\prime}}}=\sigma\omega^{\prime}\omega
. Then, if we were to start with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, we would eventually arrive at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma\omega^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
(without ever having looked at an empty stack) and thus we would also get an infinite sequence of type identifiers.

In conclusion, a system is non-contractive iff one of the following properties hold:

•

there is a variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
such that, when starting from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
and following trivial rewriting rules, one returns to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
;

•

there is a variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}
and a stack symbol
\sigma
such that, when starting from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
and following trivial rewriting rules, one reaches
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma\omega}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
for some (possibly empty) stack contents
\omega
, without ever observing an empty stack.

Next, we show that these properties can be decidable in polynomial time. Here we only sketch the algorithm for the case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
as the case
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is similar. Starting from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, we construct a sequence of type identifiers
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
by following the rewriting rules, and additionally applying some shortcutting which we describe in a moment. At stage
i
, we have produced the type identifier
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
; let
\sigma_{i}
be the top stack symbol of
\omega_{i}
. We produce the next term in the sequence as follows.

•

Suppose
\sigma_{i}=\varepsilon
, i.e., we have reached the empty stack. Then we can immediately end the procedure and exclude
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
from being the start of an infinite sequence that never observes an empty stack.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the left-hand side of a non-trivial equation. Then we can immediately end the procedure, and correctly assert that no infinite sequence of rewriting rules could start with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the left-hand side of a trivial equation (with
\sigma_{i}\neq\varepsilon
), and that this is the first time in the sequence that we have observed this combination of variable and top stack symbol. Then we continue with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i+1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i+1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
by following the appropriate rewriting rule.

•

Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the left-hand side of a trivial equation (with
\sigma_{i}\neq\varepsilon
), but it repeats a previous combination. That is, we have previously seen
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i^{\prime}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}
and
\sigma_{i}
being the top stack symbol of
\omega_{i^{\prime}}
. Let
i^{\prime}
be the last time this combination was observed. Now suppose that, between
i^{\prime}
and
i
, the stack length has never dropped below
|\omega_{i^{\prime}}|
; in particular, this implies that
|\omega_{i^{\prime}}|\leq|\omega_{i}|
. Then, we can immediately end the sequence and correctly assert that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
(and hence, also
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
) leads to an infinite sequence of rewriting rules.

•

Suppose again that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
is the left-hand side of a trivial equation (with
\sigma_{i}\neq\varepsilon
), whose combination was seen before for the last time at
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. But now suppose that, at some point between
i^{\prime}
and
i
, the stack length has dropped below
|\omega_{i^{\prime}}|
. Let
j
be the very first time it did so, which means that
i^{\prime}<j\leq i
and in particular that
\omega_{j}
is the stack obtained by popping the top symbol
\sigma
from
\omega_{i^{\prime}}
. Now, if we were to follow the rewriting rules from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, we would essentially repeat the same rules from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i^{\prime}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{j}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. Hence, we can shortcut this part and produce the next term
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i+1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega_{i+1}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{i+1}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X_{j}}}
and
\omega_{i+1}
as
\omega_{i}
without the top symbol
\sigma_{i}
. Notice that each time we apply this shortcut, the stack length necessarily decreases.

Next, we need to show that our procedure eventually ends after polynomially many iterations. We can terminate our procedure either by observing a non-trivial equation, or by observing an empty stack, or by repeating a previous combination of variable and top stack symbol that provably yields an infinite sequence. Notice that, each time a combination of variable and top stack symbol is repeated, either we terminate the procedure (asserting the existence of an infinite sequence) or the next term in the sequence has a smaller stack length. Hence, we can only increase the stack length at most
|Q||\Delta|-1
times (once for each different combination of variable and top stack symbol, excluding the initial term). Therefore, if after
2|Q||\Delta|
iterations we have not yet ended the procedure, then we must reach an empty stack. Thus the number of iterations is
\mathcal{O}(|Q||\Delta|)
. We also need to argue that each iteration can be done in constant time.
For most cases, this is obvious, as we merely have to lookup the right-hand side of the equation corresponding to the currently known symbol. The only case which is not obvious is when we apply the shorcutting rule. But here, we can use a lookup table that saves, for each combination
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, whether the sequence starting from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
eventually drops to an empty stack, and if so, to which variable does it lead. We can do this by, at each iteration and for each
n
, keeping track of the set of configurations
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, such that
\omega
has size
n
and the stack length has not dropped below
n
since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\omega}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
was last visited.

To conclude, we can decide whether a system is contractive by applying the above procedure for every choice of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\varepsilon}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
. As there are only
|Q|(|\Delta|+1)
(i.e., polynomially many) choices, the total running time would be
\mathcal{O}(|Q|^{2}|\Delta|^{2})
. This can be further reduced to
\mathcal{O}(|Q||\Delta|)
since, after following the procedure above for a given choice of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, we can infer whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
leads to an infinite sequence for all the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
that were visited, and thus exclude them from our subsequent analysis. In other words, we can keep a lookup table that says, for every
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
, whether it has been discovered before, and prune our search the next time we revisit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{X}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\langle}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\sigma}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rangle}}
.
∎
